Computing f-Divergences and Distances of High-Dimensional Probability Density Functions — Low-Rank Tensor Approximations —
Alexander Litvinenko, Youssef Marzouk, Hermann G. Matthies, Marco Scavino, Alessio Spantini

Abstract
Very often, in the course of uncertainty quantification tasks or data analysis, one has to deal with high-dimensional random variables. Here the interest is mainly to compute characterisations like the entropy, the Kullback-Leibler divergence, more general f f -divergences, or other such characteristics based on the probability density. The density is often not available directly, and it is a computational challenge to just represent it in a numerically feasible fashion in case the dimension is even moderately large. It is an even stronger numerical challenge to then actually compute said characteristics in the high-dimensional case. In this regardit is proposed to approximate the discretised density in a compressed form, in particular by a low-rank tensor. This can alternatively be obtained from the corresponding probability characteristic function, or more general representations of the underlying random variable. The mentioned characterisations need point-wise functions like the logarithm. This normally rather trivial task becomes computationally difficult when the density is approximated in a compressed resp. low-rank tensor format, as the point values are not directly accessible. The computations become possible by considering the compressed data as an element of an associative, commutative algebra with an inner product, and using matrix algorithms to accomplish the mentioned tasks. The representation as a low-rank element of a high order tensor space allows to reduce the computational complexity and storage cost from exponential in the dimension to almost linear. Keywords: high-dimensional probability density, Kullback-Leibler divergence, f-divergence , distance , tensor representation, computational algorithms , low-rank approximation MSC Classification: 41A05 ⋅ \cdot\; 41A45 ⋅ \cdot\; 41A63 ⋅ \cdot\; 60-08 ⋅ \cdot\; 60E07 ⋅ \cdot\; 60E10 ⋅ \cdot\; 62-08 ⋅ \cdot\; 62E17 ⋅ \cdot\; 62H10 ⋅ \cdot\; 65C20 ⋅ \cdot\; 65F55 ⋅ \cdot\; 65F60 ⋅ \cdot\; 94A17



1
Introduction

In statistics and probability, and in particular in the vigorous field of
uncertainty quantification (UQ)
[
44
,
88
,
87
]
,
one often has to deal with high-dimensional random variables (RVs) with values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Frequently, one is interested in characteristic quantities of interest (QoI), examples are
the differential entropy, the Kullback-Leibler or more general
f
-divergences,
or other such characteristics based on integrals of some function of the probability density function (
pdf
).
Computing such integrals, or just storing a discretised form of the
pdf
, may become a
daunting task for even moderately high dimensions
d
.

1.1
Motivation and main idea

One apparently quite efficient way to deal with such high-dimensional
RVs is to view them as elements of some tensor product space of tensors of
high order — typically the order corresponds to the dimension
d

[
55
]
.
Such tensors can be approximated by low-rank tensors, and thus it becomes feasible to
deal with them numerically.
Here we concentrate solely
on real valued continuous RVs which possess a
pdf
,
so another possibility is the
representation of the
pdf
in some computationally advantageous form.

Possible QoIs are often
defined or only accessible in some specific representation of the RV.
We are thinking of QoIs such as the
entropy, the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, or more generally
f
-divergences,
as well as
Hellinger distances, and (central) and generalised moments, to name a few such QoIs

[
82
,
90
,
112
]
.
Some are defined and can efficiently be computed with
a functional representation of the RV, and some are defined and efficiently computed
if one has access to the point values of the
pdf
, the case to be considered here.

The algorithms we formulate are for a compressed discrete representation of the
pdf
, and
we actually use for our computations a low-rank tensor approximation.
We sketch three possibilities how to arrive at such a low-rank
representation of the density. One is via tensor function representations

[
13
,
8
,
55
,
48
,
15
]
of the
pdf

[
30
,
103
]
,
another via an analogous representation of the probabilistic characteristic function
(
pcf
)
[
110
,
106
,
120
]
, and a third one
is via a low-rank representation of the RV itself—e.g.
[
23
,
24
]
.

Connecting and connected with these different representations—next to the
already mentioned
pcf
—are other well known characterising objects like the
moment generating function or
the second characteristic or cumulant generating function, and we show how these may be
efficiently computed as well in a low-rank tensor format.
Motivating factors for an accuracy controlled low-rank tensor compression include the following

[
78
,
55
]
:

•

reduce the storage cost to linear or even sub-linear in the dimension
d
;

•

perform algebraic operations in cost linear or even sub-linear in the dimension
d
;

•

combining it with the fast Fourier transform (FFT) yields a
superfast

FT
[
92
,
27
]
.

On the other hand, general limitations of such a tensor compression technique are that

•

it could be time-consuming to compute a low-rank tensor decomposition;

•

with sampling or point evaluation, it requires an axes-parallel mesh;

•

after algebraic manipulations, a re-compression may be necessary;

It is a fact that there are situations where storage of
all items is not feasible, or operating on all items is out of the question,
so that some kind of compression has to be employed, and
low-rank tensor techniques offer a very promising avenue
[
78
,
55
]
.
But for the algorithms shown later in Section
5
all that is required is that the compressed
data can be considered
as an element of an associative, commutative algebra with an inner product.

Further, the actual computational representation is allowed to be a lossy compression, and
the algebraic operations may be performed in an approximate fashion, so
as to maintain a high compression level. We address explicitly
the representation of data as a tensor with compression in the form of a
low-rank representation.
The suggested technique, in order to be employed efficiently, assumes that all involved
tensors have a low rank, and the rank is not increasing strongly after linear algebra operations.
In Section
6
we show some exemplary numerical examples of
applications of this framework to approximate high-dimensional
pdfs
of

\alpha
-stable type—which are given only through their
pcf
—and compute
divergences and distances between such probability distributions.

Let us give an example which motivates much of the following formulation and development.
Assume that one is dealing with a random vector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
in a high-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{d})^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
defined on a suitable probability space
\Omega
. Further assume that this random vector
has a
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, and that we
want to compute—as a simple example of the possible tasks envisioned—the

differential entropy
(see also Eq. (
37
)), the

expectation
of the negative logarithm of the
pdf
. This requires the
point-wise logarithm of
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
:

h(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}):=\mathbb{E}\left(-\ln(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}))\right):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\ln(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}))p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}.

(1)

Even if one has an analytical expression resp. approximation
for the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, it still may not be
possible to compute the above integral analytically, and so we propose to do this
numerically. This immediately provokes the
curse of dimensionality
, as the
numerical evaluation of such high dimensional integrals can be very expensive, with
work proportional
n^{d}
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
is a discretisation parameter .
In the following, we shall propose one
possible approach to alleviate the computational burden.

The goal is a discrete low-rank tensor point evaluation of the
pdf
. The first
starting point is the assumption that one has or may obtain
a low-rank tensor function representation

[
13
,
8
,
55
,
48
,
15
]
of the
pdf

[
30
,
103
]
. The second possible starting point in is that one has such a low-rank tensor function of the
pcf

[
86
,
110
,
106
,
120
,
10
]
.
Then via the Fourier transform one may come back to the
pdf
. And the third possibility
sketched is that one has a sparse or low-rank representation of a
high-dimensional RV. From this one may evaluate the
pcf
on a
tensor grid, and from there then via the Fourier transform again arrive at a low-rank
approximation of the
pdf
.

Further assume that the
pdf
of the high-dimensional RV
has its support in a compact hyper-rectangle,

\supp\,p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}:=\cl\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\neq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}\}\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\raise-2.20001pt\hbox{\huge$\times$}}{\raise-0.50003pt\hbox{\Large$\times$}}{\hbox{\large$\times$}}{\times}}_{\nu=1}^{d}[\xi_{\nu}^{(\min)},\xi_{\nu}^{(\max)}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
A fully discrete representation of the
pdf
can be achieved (further details will be
provided in Section
2
) by picking
in each dimension
1\leq\nu\leq d
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
an equidistant grid vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}_{\nu}:=(\hat{x}_{1,\nu},\dots,\hat{x}_{M_{\nu},\nu})
of size
M_{\nu}
, such that for all
\nu
it holds that

\hat{x}_{i_{\nu},\nu}\in[\xi_{\nu}^{(\min)},\xi_{\nu}^{(\max)}]
for
1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu}
.
The size
M_{\nu}
could be different for each
dimension
\nu
, but for the sake of simplicity here we shall often assume them all equal to
n
,
i.e. each
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The whole grid will be denoted by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\raise-2.20001pt\hbox{\huge$\times$}}{\raise-0.50003pt\hbox{\Large$\times$}}{\hbox{\large$\times$}}{\times}}_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}_{\nu}=(\hat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{X}}})_{(\nu,i_{1},\dots,i_{d})}

with
1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu}
.

The notation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}:=p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})
will denote the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\in\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}=:\mathcal{T}=(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{N}
, with

\dim\mathcal{T}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}M_{\nu}=:N=n^{d}
, the components of which are
the evaluation of the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
on the grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}:=p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}):=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}):=(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\hat{x}_{i_{1},1},\dots,\hat{x}_{i_{d},d})),\quad 1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu},\;1\leq\nu\leq d.

(2)

If
n
and especially the dimension
d
are even moderately large, the total dimension

N=n^{d}
is very quickly a huge number, maybe even so that it is not possible to
reasonably store that amount of information.

Sometimes the density
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
is—at least approximately—given as an analytical
expression, and it may be possible to approximate it
[
59
]
through
a low-rank function tensor representation. In the simplest case this would look like

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\approx\tilde{p}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}p_{\ell,\nu}(y_{\nu}),

(3)

where each
p_{\ell,\nu}
is only a function of the real variable
y_{\nu}
in dimension
\nu
.
This so-called
canonical polyadic
(CP) tensor representation
[
59
]

(cf. Section
4
) becomes
computationally viable when the
rank

R
can be chosen fairly small.
The multi-dimensional Gaussian distribution with diagonal covariance matrix is an
obvious simple case in point with
R=1
.
The
p_{\ell,\nu}
are evaluated on the grid vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}_{\nu}

for all
\nu
and
\ell
, giving

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{\ell,\nu}:=(p_{\ell,\nu}(\hat{x}_{1,\nu}),\dots,p_{\ell,\nu}(\hat{x}_{M_{\nu},\nu}))\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}.

This is a building block for a possible low-rank CP representation of the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
,
as now

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\approx\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{\ell,\nu}.

(4)

More details and descriptions about this and other low-rank tensor approximations will be
given in Section
4
.
To evaluate now numerically an expression like the differential entropy
Eq. (
1
), the integral is replaced by a numerical quadrature

h(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}})\approx\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{M_{1}}\cdots\sum_{i_{d}=1}^{M_{d}}-\ln(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}w_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}},

(5)

where
w_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}
are integration weights, which will all be chosen equal

w_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}\propto N^{-1}
, cf. Section
2
and Section
3
.

The challenge in Eq. (
5
)
is the huge number of terms in the sum. Here the low-rank representation Eq. (
4
)
can be used to advantage, cf. Section
2
, but then the challenge is to compute
the logarithm in Eq. (
5
) when
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
is in
the representation Eq. (
4
). For this certain algebraic properties of the
space
\mathcal{T}
will be used, as will be explained in Section
2
and Section
5
.
There are of course other ways to arrive at a discrete low-rank representation of a
high-dimensional function. One group of such possible methods are the various
“cross”-procedures
[
36
,
99
,
8
,
7
,
26
,
31
]
. In any case, this is
not supposed to be an exhaustive survey of such methods, and only intended
to give some hints and point to some possibilities.

At other times the
pdf
may not be available, but instead the probabilistic characteristic
function (
pcf
)
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
of the
pdf

\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}):=\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\right\rangle)\right):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\right\rangle)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}=:\mathcal{F}_{d}(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}})(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}),

(6)

where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}=(t_{1},t_{2},...,t_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the dual variable to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\right\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}y_{j}t_{j}
is the canonical inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and

\mathcal{F}_{d}
is the probabilist’s
d
-dimensional Fourier transform (FT) Eq. (
6
),
may be given
[
10
]
, at least
approximately, as an analytical expression
(similar to Eq. (
3
))

\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})\approx\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\varphi_{\ell,\nu}(t_{\nu}),

(7)

where the
\varphi_{\ell,\nu}(t_{\nu})
are one-dimensional functions. Examples of
such a situation are
elliptically contoured
\alpha
-stable distributions
, or also

symmetric infinitely divisible distributions

[
10
]
.
From this it may be deduced that an approximate low-rank expression of the
pdf
is given by (cf. Eq. (
3
))

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\approx\tilde{p}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}){\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{1}(\varphi_{\ell,\nu})(y_{\nu}),

(8)

where
\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{1}
is the
one-dimensional
probabilist’s inverse FT.

For the discrete grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}
, there is a corresponding
dual grid

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}=(\hat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{T}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}})_{(\nu,i_{1},\dots,i_{d})}
with
1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu}

for the discrete Fourier transform
[
16
]
of same size and dimensions. Similarly to the
pdf
evaluated on the grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}

and represented by the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
,
the
pcf
will be used in a discrete setting evaluated on this dual grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}:=\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}):=(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\hat{t}_{i_{1},1},\dots,\hat{t}_{i_{d},d}))=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}),\quad 1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu},\;1\leq\nu\leq d.

(9)

From Eq. (
7
) it is now easy to see that, as in Eq. (
4
),

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}\approx\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\ell,\nu},

where the vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\ell,\nu}
are the evaluations of the one-dimensional
functions
\varphi_{\ell,\nu}
from Eq. (
7
), given by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\ell,\nu}:=(\varphi_{\ell,\nu}(\hat{t}_{1,\nu}),\dots,\varphi_{\ell,\nu}(\hat{t}_{M_{\nu},\nu}))\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}.

From these vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\ell,\nu}
one may now compute with the discrete one-dimensional
inverse Fourier transform, for the sake of simplicity again denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{1}
,
the vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{\ell,\nu}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\ell,\nu})
, such that from
Eq. (
8
) one arrives at an expression corresponding to Eq. (
4
):

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}})\approx\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\ell,\nu})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{\ell,\nu}.

(10)

Thus one again obtains a numerical low-rank representation for the density
as in Eq. (
4
). Obviously, the cross-methods

[
36
,
99
,
8
,
7
,
26
,
31
]
alluded
to there can be used here too, to directly obtain a
low-rank representation of the
pcf
. Then again, with the help of the discrete
inverse Fourier transforms, one arrives at a low-rank representation of the
pdf
.

Random vectors of high dimension
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
occur also when random fields resp. stochastic
processes are discretised, often given through their
so-called Karhunen-Loève expansion (KLE)

[
84
,
67
,
68
]
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, truncated at
r\in\mathbb{N}
:

{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\omega)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}\lambda_{\ell}^{1/2}\,\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{\ell}.

(11)

This places the random vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega)=[\dots,\xi_{k}(\omega),\dots]
, a function of the two variables
(\omega,k)
, in the
tensor product
\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and the Karhunen-Loève expansion Eq. (
11
)
(another form of singular value decomposition)
is a
separated
representation of this tensor of second order.
Often the singular values
\lambda_{\ell}^{1/2}

decay quickly as
\ell
grows, so that one may obtain a good approximation
with only
r
terms.

To see in a nutshell where this leads to, assume further that the uncorrelated
RVs
\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)=\sum_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\zeta_{\ell}^{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}\Psi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\omega))

may be expanded in Wiener’s polynomial chaos expansion (PCE),
see e.g.
[
66
]
,
with multi-variate polynomials
\Psi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\omega)):=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\psi_{\alpha_{j}}(\theta_{j}(\omega))
in
iid

standard normalised Gaussians
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\omega)=(\theta_{1}(\omega),\dots,\theta_{d}(\omega))
,
where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})
is a multi-index and the

\psi_{\alpha_{j}}(\theta_{j}(\omega))
are uni-variate polynomials.
Inserting this into the KLE, one obtains a combined truncated KLE / PCE

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega)\approx\sum_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}\Psi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\omega))
,
where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}\lambda_{\ell}^{1/2}\,\zeta_{\ell}^{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Arguably, now the tensor—a multi-dimensional array—

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}=(\xi_{k}^{(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})})
represents the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.
Such a tensor can—as before—be represented in the
canonical polyadic
(CP)
format
[
59
]
, say with low
CP-rank

R
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}\approx\sum_{r=1}^{R}\bigotimes_{k=0}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}^{(k)}_{r}=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}_{r}
.

Having
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}
in a low-rank representation then leads to a
low-rank representation of the random vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}})
,
to obtain a formula for a quick evaluation of the
pcf
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}})
:

\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}})\right)
, yielding the
pcf
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}})
in a low-rank tensor function format.
Now we are back to the situation as before when the
pcf
was assumed given.

1.2
Literature review and outline of the paper

To compute quantities of interest (QoIs) such as the
f
-divergences—see Section
3
for the distances and divergences considered—functions such
as the square root, the logarithm, etc., have to be computed point-wise
on the
pdf
. But in a compressed format the point values are not directly accessible.
An important contribution of this paper is to show that such computations are still
possible efficiently by operating directly on the compressed format. This will be enabled
by identifying both the
pcf
and the
pdf
as elements of algebras. A similar idea
was already used in
[
33
,
39
]

for post-processing low-rank representations of RVs, and
here it is extended to densities and characteristic functions. It is well-known
that algebras can be represented as linear operators resp. matrices in the finite-dimensional
case (e.g.
[
109
]
), and one can employ the spectral calculus of linear
operators in order to obtain these point-wise evaluations in a low-rank format.
In the concrete case here this boils down to using algorithms which were developed to
compute functions of matrices
[
64
]
.

Therefore, special compressed / low-rank data structures are needed.
The CP format used above for illustrative purposes may be the simplest, but
we suggest to use the low-rank
tensor train (TT) data format
[
99
,
100
]
. Other known
tensor formats, such as: Tucker, and hierarchical Tucker (HT)
could be also applied
[
55
,
70
,
75
,
12
]
.
For the sake of simplicity of exposition, the CP tensor format is considered
in the main part of the paper for illustrative purposes, when direct reference to a low-rank format is
necessary. The computations are also possible in other compressed formats. In
[
39
]
the computations
of the operations of the algebra were already given for various tensor formats, and they
may also be found e.g. in
[
55
,
70
,
75
,
12
]
, so that
we may be brief here. For numerical experiments we use the Tensor Train
(TT) software library TT-tool
[
100
]
.

In this work we would like to explore the usefulness and applicability of these
techniques in probability. In probability theory, given a
d
-dimensional RV,
it is well known that its characterisation is provided by the
pcf

[
86
]
(Feller and Lévi theorems). Therefore, our contribution will also focus
on approximation techniques for representing the
pcf
in low-rank
tensor format. Previous approaches to approximate the
pcf
were
not able to handle high-dimensional RVs, e.g.
[
86
,
110
,
120
]
.
Our approach can overcome these difficulties.
Other numerical methods which rely on the use of the
pcf
may also benefit from the
proposed approach; e.g. the idea to use Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods in the
Fourier domain to sample from a density proportional to the absolute value of the
underlying characteristic function is presented in
[
10
]
.

In a very recent paper

[
103
]
, the authors estimate tensor train (TT) ranks for approximated multivariate
Gaussian
pdfs
. In
[
30
]
, the authors use the TT-format to approximate
multivariate probability distributions. There they analyse properties of the obtained
low-rank approximation and use it as a prior distribution in the MCMC
approach. In
[
120
,
121
]
, the author
develops a numerical inversion of characteristic functions.

Low-rank tensor techniques proved to be very successful in such areas as numerical
mathematics
[
39
,
33
,
80
,
75
,
55
,
12
]
,
computational chemistry
[
70
]
, statistics
[
83
]

and others
[
54
]
. Many known types of Green’s functions were
approximated in the low-rank tensor format
[
75
,
73
,
74
]
, which resulted in drastically reduced computational cost
and storage.

General tensor formats and their low-rank approximations, in quantum physics
also known as
tensor networks
, are described in
[
118
,
105
,
41
,
93
,
18
,
14
]
.
For the mathematical and numerical point of view we refer to
the review
[
78
]
, the monographs
[
59
,
70
,
75
]
,
and to the literature survey on low-rank approximations
[
54
]
.

Some of the necessary theory is reviewed in Section
2
. The discrete versions of the
pdf
and the
pcf
are represented in a compressed approximation,
we propose to use low-rank tensor approximations.
The discretised
pdf
and
pcf
are viewed as tensors equipped with a discrete
version of the point-wise product, the so-called Hadamard product. With this product
they become (commutative) C
∗
-algebras, so it is possible to define algebraically
the desired functions on these tensors, and on their compressed approximation,
through the use of matrix algebra algorithms
[
64
]
.

Assuming that the
pdf
has been represented in such an algebraic setting,
in Section
3
we explain how to compute statistical moments and divergences in
this discretised framework, using only the abstract discrete operations, independent
of any particular representation. In Section
4
we show as an example how
the algebraic operations may be actually implemented on a low-rank tensor format, namely first
for illustrative purposes on
the simpler canonical polyadic (CP) tensor decomposition, where we
recall all required definitions and properties. This tensor format
is the easiest one to explain the ideas.
In the example computations later in Section
6
we actually use the tensor-train
(TT) format, but here the explanation is more complicated.
The actual algorithms—developed for matrices—which can be used
to compute various functions of
pdfs
are either iterations or
truncated series expansions, and are listed in Section
5
. Again, only the abstract discrete operations are needed, and the algorithms are independent
of any particular representation.
In Section
6
some numerical examples are given, and Section
7
concludes.

2
Theoretical background

The two entities we will be working with are the probability density function (
pdf
)
and the corresponding probability characteristic function (
pcf
). These are connected
by the Fourier transform. The ultimate goal is to use discretised versions of these
two descriptors of RVs to compute for example the
pdf
of a mixture model
or the
pdf
of the sum of two independent RVs, or quantities of interest (QoIs)
like moments, or other statistics like the relative entropy of a RV, or—for two

pdfs
—their Kullback-Leibler (KL) or more general
f
-divergences. While introducing
the notation, this section serves to collect the basic properties of the
different descriptors such as moments,
cumulative distribution function
cdf
,
pdf
,
pcf
,
second characteristic or cumulant generating function
[
106
,
86
,
120
]
, and the moment generating function.
The discretised versions of these QoIs, properties and operations will later be used in the
numerical computations.
In order to evaluate
point-wise functions of the
pdf
, which is necessary to compute various QoIs, one has to view the
pdf
as well
as the
pcf
as elements of function algebras, something which has to be conserved by the
discretisation and low-rank representation.

2.1
Notation and summary of basic descriptors

The random vector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}:\Omega\to\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
a measurable mapping w.r.t. the Borel-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P})
,
where
\mathfrak{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra and
\mathbb{P}
a probability measure was already
introduced in Section
1
.
We are mainly interested in the case where
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{d}

is a high-dimensional vector space. The associated expectation operator
is denoted by
\mathbb{E}\left(\cdot\right)
, the mean as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}:=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right)=\int_{\Omega}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega)\,\mathbb{P}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and the mean zero random part as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}
.
The canonical Euclidean inner product

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}:=\sum_{k=1}^{d}x_{k}y_{k}
on
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
is used
to identify the space
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
with its dual.
The usual order structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathcal{V}
is assumed, so that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\leq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}
iff
x_{k}\leq y_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}=\mathcal{V}_{+}
,
with positive cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}=\mathcal{V}_{+}=\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x_{1}\geq 0,\dots,x_{d}\geq 0\}
.

The
moments

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k}
and the
central moments

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}_{k}
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}

of order
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
—assuming they exist—are basic descriptors of a RV and are denoted as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k}=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\otimes k}\right)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k},\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}_{k}=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}^{\otimes k}\right)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}.

(12)

where for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
one sets
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes k}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
.
The second central moment—the
covariance
matrix—is also denoted as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}=\cov{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Xi}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}\in\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}=(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes 2}
.
If
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is another random vector with values in
\mathcal{U}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the

mixed
and
mixed central
moments are denoted by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}_{k,\ell}=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\otimes\ell}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}_{k,\ell}=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\eta}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\eta}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\eta}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\eta}}}^{\otimes\ell}\right)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\ell}.

(13)

The
covariance
is also denoted as
\cov(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}_{1,1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Y}}_{1,1}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\eta}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\eta}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\eta}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\eta}}}\in\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{U}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The distribution measure

P_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
for a Borel subset
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}

is as usual the
push-forward

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}\mathbb{P}

of the original measure
\mathbb{P}
:

P_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathcal{E}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}):=\mathbb{P}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}(\mathcal{E}))=\mathbb{E}\left({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}})\right),
where the
characteristic indicator function

{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
is unity if
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathcal{E}
and
vanishes otherwise, and is assumed absolutely continuous w.r.t. Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This leads to an absolutely continuous
cumulative distribution function
(
cdf
)
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
—defined for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathcal{V}
via semi-infinite intervals

\mathcal{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\raise-2.20001pt\hbox{\huge$\times$}}{\raise-0.50003pt\hbox{\Large$\times$}}{\hbox{\large$\times$}}{\times}}_{k=1}^{d}\;]\!-\!\infty,y_{k}]\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}):=P_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}})=\mathbb{P}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\leq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathbb{E}\left({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}})\right).

(14)

The well known properties of
F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
such as positivity
F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\geq 0
,
and the monotonicity

F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}_{1})\leq F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}_{2})
for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}\leq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}_{2}
, as well as
F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\to 0
as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\to-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}
and
F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\to 1
as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\to+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\infty}}
, should
be replicated in any discretised setting. As
P_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
is absolutely continuous,
it has a Radon-Nikodým derivative
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}P_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})

w.r.t. Lebesgue measure, the
probability density function
(
pdf
):

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}}P_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\frac{\mathop{}\!\partial^{d}}{\mathop{}\!\partial y_{1}\dots\mathop{}\!\partial y_{d}}F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}});\;\text{ with }p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\geq 0,\;\text{ and }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=1.

(15)

These defining relations for the
pdf
are directly implied
by the properties of the
cdf

F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, and are important
to be preserved under discretisation and compressed low-rank approximation.
The positivity in Eq. (
15
) means that geometrically
speaking densities are in the positive convex
cone of
\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, and the integral relation means that they lie on a
hyperplane. The intersection of these two closed convex sets is denoted by

\mathfrak{D}:=\{p\mid p\geq 0\}\cap\{p\mid\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=1\}\subset\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}),\quad\text{ closed and convex.}

(16)

In addition one notes that
\left\|p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right\|_{1}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\right|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=1
, i.e.
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
is on the unit sphere.
Convexity of
\mathfrak{D}
in Eq. (
16
) means that convex
combinations of densities
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1}},\dots,p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{m}}\in\mathfrak{D}
are again densities; corresponding to
mixture models
.

Direct
quantities of interest
(QoIs) are usually expected values of functions of

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, i.e. quantities like
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{g}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{g}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{g}}=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}})\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}=\mathcal{Y}
. Obviously the moments and central moments Eq. (
12
)
are special cases of this by taking as QoI
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{k}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes k}
,
as is the characteristic function Eq. (
6
).
These quantities may alternatively be computed by integrating over the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
like Eq. (
1
) in Section
1
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{g}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{g}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{g}}=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}})\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}F_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=:\mathbb{E}_{p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}})\in\mathcal{Y}.

(17)

As is well known (e.g.
[
109
]
), the Banach space
\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
is
a commutative Banach algebra when the space is equipped with the convolution product:

(p*q)(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\quad\text{ for }p,q\in\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}).

(18)

Observe that if
p
and
q
are density functions, so is
p*q
. This
reflects the well known fact that if
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
is the density of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, and

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}}
is the density of the independent RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, then
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}*p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}}
is the
density of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}
. It also means that the closed convex set
\mathfrak{D}
in
Eq. (
16
) is stable under convolution.

The
characteristic function
(
pcf
) of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
stated already in Eq. (
6
)
may be seen defined as a QoI via Eq. (
17
) with

g_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)
, i.e. the probabilist’s Fourier transform—in
other fields this is considered as the
non-unitary version of the
inverse
Fourier transform
[
16
]
—of the
pdf
.
Well known
[
86
,
120
]
facts about characteristic
functions
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}

are that they are bounded and uniformly continuous—
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\in\mathrm{C}_{bu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})

—and satisfy
\left|\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})\right|\leq\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(0)=1

for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.
\left\|\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right\|_{\infty}=1
.
These conditions mean that
pcfs
lie on a hyperplane in the
vector space
\mathrm{C}_{bu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
, obviously reflecting the hyperplane condition—unit
integral—for the
pdf
.

The
pcf
is a complex-valued function, but as
the Fourier transform of
the real and positive
pdf
, it has to satisfy some further constraints.
To be able to properly formulate this, one
defines an anti-linear involution or
\star
-operation “*” as

\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{*}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}):=\bar{\varphi}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})
,
where the overbar denotes the complex conjugate. Now, as the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
in Eq. (
6
) is a real function, this implies

[
16
,
86
]
that
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}

is Hermitean, i.e. invariant w.r.t. the conjugation induced by the *-involution;
so it satisfies
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{*}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\bar{\varphi}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})
.
This real subspace of Hermitean functions will be denoted by

\mathcal{H}\subset\mathrm{C}_{bu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
.

As is well known
[
109
]
, the Banach space
\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})

together with point-wise multiplication and the
\star
-operation is a commutative C*-algebra,
and the real subspace
\mathcal{H}
of Hermitean functions is a
real

sub-algebra.
As
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
is non-negative
[
86
]
, the
pcf

\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
has to be a positive definite function: for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
distinct points
\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}_{k=1}^{n}
, the matrix

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}=(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}_{i}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}_{j}))_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}

is Hermitean positive semi-definite in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The positive definite
functions form a convex cone in
\mathcal{H}
, reflecting the positivity of the
pdf
.
These conditions will not be so easy to directly ascertain on a discretised
and compressed low-rank representation of
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, and are simpler checked on the

pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
. All this means that the
pcf
is in the intersection of
this cone
\mathcal{H}
and the hyperplane of functions with unit value at the origin in
\mathcal{H}
,
again a closed and convex set, denoted by

\mathfrak{C}=\mathcal{H}\cap\{\varphi\mid\varphi(0)=1\}\cap\{\varphi\mid\varphi\text{ is positive semi-definite }\}\subset\mathrm{C}_{bu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C}).

(19)

Thus, real convex combinations of
pcfs
are again
pcf
, namely of mixture models,
reflecting the analogous property for
pdfs
in
\mathfrak{D}
in
Eq. (
16
).

It is also easily seen that the point-wise product of two
pcfs
is again a
pcf
, and hence the set
\mathfrak{C}
in Eq. (
19
) is stable under
point-wise products. This reflects the fact that the point-wise product
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\cdot\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}}
of the
pcfs
of two independent RVs
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is just the
pcf
of their sum.
This is the analogue to the previous statement about the convolution of two
pdfs
,
showing that
\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathfrak{D})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}
.

2.2
Relations through the Fourier transform

The Fourier transform is known
[
16
,
86
]
to connect the
algebraic convolution structure on probability densities described previously
with the corresponding multiplication structure on characteristic functions.
Let
p,q\in\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
be integrable real-valued functions in the
convolution Banach-algebra, and denote their Fourier transforms (FT in Eq. (
6
))
by
\phi=\mathcal{F}_{d}(p),\psi=\mathcal{F}_{d}(q)\in\mathcal{H}
(as
p,q
are real, their FT is Hermitean),
then the Fourier transform and its inverse
\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}
has the following
well known
[
16
,
86
]
property:

\mathcal{F}_{d}(p*q)=\mathcal{F}_{d}(p)\cdot\mathcal{F}_{d}(q)=\phi\cdot\psi\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad p*q=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\phi\cdot\psi).

(20)

Thus the Fourier
transform (FT) is a real algebra homomorphism between the real convolution Banach-algebra

\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and the real multiplication algebra
\mathcal{H}
of Hermitean functions.

In our context, further well known
[
16
,
109
]

properties of the FT, which will be needed later, are
embodied in the statement
\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathfrak{D})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}
at the end of Subsection
2.1
,
can be summarised as follows: there is a correspondence between real-valued functions in
\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and
Hermitean Fourier transforms
\mathcal{H}
, and the positive convex cone in
