\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
corresponds to the convex cone of
positive-definite functions in
\mathcal{H}
, while
the hyperplane with unit integral in
\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
corresponds with the hyperplane with
unit value at the origin in
\mathcal{H}
. These relations will be used in the discretised
low-rank setting in the following Subsection
2.3
to ascertain the correctness
of the approximations. This means that as the
pdf
satisfies
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\in\mathfrak{D}
in
Eq. (
16
), so the discretised version will have to satisfy a similar
discrete constraint, and dually, as the
pcf
satisfies
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\in\mathfrak{C}
in
Eq. (
19
), the discretised version of this
quantity will have to satisfy a similar discrete constraint.

Another well known property
[
16
,
109
]
of the Fourier transform that
will be needed is how it connects derivatives and multiplication by the co-ordinates. We assume that all derivatives appearing in the sequel exist and are well defined.
In fact, from Eq. (
6
) one gleans that

(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\mathop{}\!\partial_{t_{k}})\,\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x_{k}\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}\left(x_{k}p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\right)(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})
.
Further, denoting the tensor of
k
-th derivatives by

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{D}^{k}\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\left(\frac{\mathop{}\!\partial^{k}}{\mathop{}\!\partial_{t_{i_{1}}}\dots\mathop{}\!\partial_{t_{i_{k}}}}\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})\right),
one obtains the well-known relation

(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}})^{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{D}^{k}\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes k}\,p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes k}\,p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\right)(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(21)

Similar relations as Eq. (
21
) can be obtained by other
characterising functions.
The
second characteristic
function
[
86
]
—sometimes
also labeled as cumulant generating function (cf. Eq. (
25
)) —whose
derivative tensors of order
k
are essentially the
cumulants

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k}

of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, is defined as the point-wise logarithm of the
pcf
:

\chi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}):=\log(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}))=\log\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rangle)\right)\right),\,\text{ with }(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}})^{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{D}^{k}\chi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}})=:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(22)

The relations Eq. (
21
) and Eq. (
22
) involve
the slightly annoying imaginary unit. To stay with completely real functions
one may switch from the Fourier transform in Eq. (
6
) or
Eq. (
22
) to the Laplace transform, at the price of working
with functions which may not be defined for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but maybe only
in a small neighbourhood around
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The
moment generating
function is defined as
[
86
]
essentially the
reflected
Laplace transform
of the density, or as evaluation of the

pcf
Eq. (
6
) for purely imaginary arguments:

M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}):=\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rangle)\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathcal{L}_{d}(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}})(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}),

(23)

where
\mathcal{L}_{d}(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}})(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\int\exp(\left\langle-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}

is the two-sided
d
-dimensional
Laplace
transform of
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
. As in
Eq. (
21
), one obtains

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{D}^{k}M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes k}\,p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(24)

Closely related is the
cumulant generating
function
[
86
]
, the
point-wise logarithm of the moment generating function
M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
in Eq. (
23
):

K_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}):=\log(M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}))=\log\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rangle)\right)\right),\,\text{ with }\mathop{}\!\mathrm{D}^{k}K_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(25)

2.3
Grid functions as tensors and algebras on them

It was already pointed out in Section
1
that we want to discretise both the

pdf
and the
pcf
, by representing them on a discrete and finite grid.
We start with the
pdf
. The first thing usually to do is to centre everything around
the mean
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}
of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, i.e. to shift co-ordinates on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}
. Another way of saying this is to state that we
work with the centred RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}
. We assume from now implicitly that this has been
done. The values of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and hence the support of its
pdf
will thus be around the origin
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The fully discrete representation of the
pdf
and the
pcf
is based on
equi-distant grid vectors

\hat{x}_{i_{\nu},\nu}=\hat{x}_{1,\nu}+(i_{\nu}-1)\Delta_{x_{\nu}}
(with increment
\Delta_{x_{\nu}}
)
of size
M_{\nu}

in each dimension
1\leq\nu\leq d
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which were
introduced in Subsection
1.1
.
The whole grid was denoted by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\raise-2.20001pt\hbox{\huge$\times$}}{\raise-0.50003pt\hbox{\Large$\times$}}{\hbox{\large$\times$}}{\times}}_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}_{\nu}
,
and we assume that it covers the support of
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
. Observe that
functions are implicitly assumed to be periodic
[
16
]
when using the FFT,
hence the total
d
-dimensional volume covered is

V=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}M_{\nu}\Delta_{x_{\nu}}
,
and the integration rule is implicitly the iterated trapezoidal rule on a periodic
grid, so each point carries the same integration weight
\scriptstyle V
/
\scriptstyle N
.

As already defined in Eq. (
2
) in Section
1
,
the notation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}:=p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})
denotes the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\in\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}=:\mathcal{T}
, a finite dimensional space with

\dim\mathcal{T}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}M_{\nu}=:N
, the components of which are
the evaluation of the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
on the grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}
, and
similarly for any other scalar function
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Similarly, any vector
valued function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}=\mathcal{Y}
, when evaluated on the grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}
,
may be viewed as a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})\in\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{T}
, and
the grid itself
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}
can be seen as an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
valued
functions evaluated on the grid.

To achieve a similar discrete representation of the
pcf
,
it was mentioned in Subsection
1.1
that one makes use of Eq. (
9
),

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})
, a discrete version of the relation Eq. (
6
),
denoting the discrete
d
-dimensional Fourier transform on the
grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}
again by
\mathcal{F}_{d}
for the sake of simplicity.
For the dual grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\raise-2.20001pt\hbox{\huge$\times$}}{\raise-0.50003pt\hbox{\Large$\times$}}{\hbox{\large$\times$}}{\times}}_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}_{\nu}
from Subsection
1.1
,
the dual grid vector in each dimension is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{t}}}_{\nu}:=(\hat{t}_{1,\nu},\dots,\hat{t}_{M_{\nu},\nu})
.
As is well known
[
16
]
, if in dimension
\nu
one has
L_{\nu}=M_{\nu}\Delta_{x_{\nu}}
as

period length
for the equi-distantly spaced primal grid with grid spacing
\Delta_{x_{\nu}}
,
then
\hat{t}_{M_{\nu},\nu}=\uppi/\Delta_{x_{\nu}}
is the highest (Nyquist)
frequency
,
and the equi-distant spacing of the dual grid in dimension
\nu
is
2\uppi/L_{\nu}
.
It is assumed
that the origin
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is in the dual grid,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}\in\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}
, and we denote
the index of the origin in the grid with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}^{0}=(j_{1}^{0},\dots,j_{d}^{0})
, i.e.
(\hat{t}_{j_{1}^{0},1},\dots,\hat{t}_{j_{d}^{0},d})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}=(0,\dots,0)
.
This point will be important in some of the statistics resp. QoIs to be
described later in Section
3
. As before,
the whole grid can be seen as an order
(d+1)
tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{T}
.
The
pcf
on the dual grid is represented as in Eq. (
9
) through the tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}:=\phi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}})\in\mathcal{T}
.
It is on these grid representations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}:=p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})
of the
pdf
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}:=\phi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}})
of the
pcf
that we propose to operate on to
compute the desired quantities of interest, e.g. the differential entropy
Eq. (
1
) in Section
1
.
A more comprehensive list of such quantities of interest is given in Section
3
.

It was already noted that even for modest values of dimension
d
and number of discretisation
points
n
the total amount of data
N=n^{d}
may become huge or even non-manageable, and one
has to resort to some kind of compressed representation resp. approximation. Here we
advocate for low-rank tensor representations to allow for efficient computation,
which will be treated in Section
4
. In such a representation it becomes difficult
to compute point-wise functions (like the log) of values of particular tensors
required for particular statistics (cf. Section
3
), as they are not accessible directly.
To still be able to do these computations, we will rely on certain algebraic properties,
cf. Section
5
. These algebraic relations have been pointed out for the non-discrete
entities in the preceding Subsection
2.2
, as well as the rôle of the Fourier transform in
connecting them. It is now important to ascertain that such algebraic relations
also hold for the grid-discrete quantities.

The set of tensors for the representations of
pdf
and
pcf
,

\mathcal{T}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{N}
is
clearly a vector space. The Section
4
will explain how the vector
operations can be numerically performed in the low-rank representation. This assures
that convex linear combinations of different
pdfs
or
pcfs
can be computed.
Furthermore, one makes
\mathcal{T}
into a Euclidean space
[
55
]

by extending the canonical inner products on the
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
onto
\mathcal{T}
; i.e. for elementary tensors

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\nu},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}_{\nu},\in\mathcal{T}
,
it is simply

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\nu}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}_{\nu}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}}
,
and then extended to all of
\mathcal{T}
by linearity.

It is convenient to extend this inner product to larger tensor products

[
55
]
, and only perform a partial inner product. For
example, if
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
is an elementary tensor in

\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{T}
, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\in\mathcal{T}
, then the partial product,
denoted as before, is

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}:=\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}=\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
and then extended to all of
\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{T}
by linearity. It is practically
a contraction over all indices related to
\mathcal{T}
.

The next task is to introduce the algebraic structures. For

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{M_{\nu}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{M_{\nu}})\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
,
we want to define the
circular convolution

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}:=(z_{1},\dots,z_{M_{\nu}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}

component-wise as

z_{k}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{M_{\nu}}r_{\ell}\,s_{m},\,m=((k-\ell)\mod{M_{\nu}})+1;\,1\leq k\leq{M_{\nu}}

[
16
,
55
]
.
This is an associative and commutative product; the convolution
algebra on
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
. It is the discrete version of the convolution algebra
\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})

considered in Subsection
2.2
. One should point out
[
16
]

that two steps are involved in going from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
resp.
\mathbb{R}
to the finite grids
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}

resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}_{\nu}
: One is the truncation of the infinite domain, say
\mathbb{R}
in dimension

\nu
, to a finite one—the interval
[\xi_{\nu}^{(\min)},\xi_{\nu}^{(\max)}]
.
This picks only certain
discrete frequencies or wavenumbers
[
16
]
from the continuum in the
Fourier transform, and they are all multiples of a basic frequency resp. wavenumber.
The second step is the
use of a finite grid, this picks a finite number of the discrete frequencies.
The effect of this is to make everything implicitly periodic, through periodic continuation.
This is reflected in the use of the
circular
convolution.

Having defined the circular convolution on each
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
, it is defined on
\mathcal{T}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
first via
elementary tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\nu},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}_{\nu}\;\in\mathcal{T}
as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}:=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\nu}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}_{\nu}
,
and extended to all of
\mathcal{T}
by linearity. This again is an associative and
commutative product; the convolution algebra on
\mathcal{T}
. In passing one may remark
that the statement that the discrete version
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}
of the
pcf
is Hermitean
positive definite is equivalent with the statement that the linear (in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
) operator

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
is Hermitean or self-adjoint
and positive definite;
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}}
is the operator of convolution with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}
.

The discrete version of the point-wise product is the Hadamard product, and again first formulated component-wise on
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
:
For
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{M_{\nu}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{M_{\nu}})\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
it is denoted by

[
55
]

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}:=(z_{1},\dots,z_{M_{\nu}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}

and defined component-wise as

z_{k}:=r_{k}\cdot s_{k},\;1\leq k\leq{M_{\nu}}
.
This also is an associative and commutative product; the Hadamard
algebra on
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
. It is again defined to
\mathcal{T}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
via elementary tensors

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\nu}
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}_{\nu}\in\mathcal{T}
as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}:=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\nu}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{s}}_{\nu}
,
and extended to all of
\mathcal{T}
by linearity. And again this is an associative and
commutative product; the Hadamard algebra on
\mathcal{T}
.
The interaction with the inner product is quite simple,
it is an elementary calculation to verify that for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}\in\mathcal{T}

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}=\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}
,
which means that the linear (in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
) operation of Hadamard multiplication by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
,
i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
, is self-adjoint.

It is easily seen that the Hadamard algebra has a multiplicative unit, which
we denote by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}=(1_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}})
— the tensor with all ones — satisfying

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
for any
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\in\mathcal{T}
. Defining

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{1}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{1}}_{\nu}:=(1,\dots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
, a vector of all ones in dimension
\nu
(which is
the Hadamard unit for the Hadamard algebra on
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
), it is
not difficult to see that the Hadamard unit on the tensor product has rank one:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{1}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{1}}_{\nu}
; it is the simple tensor product of the
Hadamard units on each
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
.

The unit further allows to introduce a discrete expectation or integral operator.
Recall that we have a tensor quadrature grid, equi-spaced in each dimension, in order to use
the FFT. As mentioned before, each point has the integration weight
\scriptstyle V
/
\scriptstyle N
.
Hence for a tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
, representing a function
p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
evaluated on the grid, the approximate integral is

\int p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\approx\mathcal{S}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}):=\frac{V}{N}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}.

(26)

Eq. (
26
) is only a convenient way of writing the approximate integral,
and obviously there is no need to actually compute the inner product with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
, i.e. multiply
each entry in the low-rank representation of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
with unity.
This Eq. (
26
) can be simply extended to discrete integrands of the
form
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}\in\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{T}
through the use of the partial inner product
defined above with the result
\mathcal{S}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}})\in\mathcal{Y}
.
If
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}
is a tensor which represents the grid-values of a function
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})
, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
represents the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
,
one may define a discrete version of the expectation, which can be
extended to other tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}\in\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{T}
, by

\mathbb{E}\left(f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}})\right)=\mathbb{E}_{p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\;\approx\;\mathcal{S}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})=\frac{V}{N}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}=:\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{F}}).

(27)

To translate the statements in
Subsection
2.2
into the present discrete setting, let
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}\in\mathcal{T}

be two tensors, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}})\in\mathcal{T}

their discrete Fourier transforms. Then one has just as in Eq. (
20
)

\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}})=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}})\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{p}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{q}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\psi}}),

(28)

showing that the discrete Fourier transform (FT) is an algebra isomorphism between the
convolution algebra
(\mathcal{T},*)
and the Hadamard algebra
(\mathcal{T},\odot)
.
This makes it relatively easy to compute the discrete convolution of the tensor
representations of two densities, say
p
represented by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
, and

q
represented by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
: Then the density
p*q
corresponds to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
,
computed with their Fourier transforms
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}})
as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Psi}})=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}))
.

For a real tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
, the Hadamard multiplication operator
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}

of Hadamard multiplication with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
was defined above. As the Hadamard
algebra is commutative, the Hadamard multiplication operators commute with each
other. Thus it is theoretically clear
[
109
]
that they can be
simultaneously diagonalised. But it is elementary to see this explicitly.
If an arbitrary tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\in\mathcal{T}
were written as a vector, the
action
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
would be the action of a
diagonal matrix, and diagonal matrices obviously commute.
This means that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
is fully diagonalised,
and the components
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{d}}
of
the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
are in fact the diagonal elements, i.e. the eigenvalues
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
. Obviously, to an eigenvalue
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{d}}

belongs the canonical unit vector with the same index as eigenvector, in tensor
notation the eigenvector is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})}=(\updelta_{(j_{1},\dots,j_{d}),(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}
,
i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{d}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})}
.

Let
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
, and let
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\in\mathcal{T}
be any tensor.
The convolution operator
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}}
of convolution with a
Hermitean
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}
was defined above. One then has

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}*\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}})\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}))=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}))=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}(\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}))),\quad\text{ or}

(29)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}})\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}*\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}))=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}))).

(30)

This shows that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
are unitarily
equivalent and have the same eigenvalues; and since those of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}

are the components
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{d}}
of the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
,
those of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}}
are the same, i.e. the components of

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}})
, the Fourier transform of the Hermitean
convolution tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}
.

When manipulating tensors which represent discrete versions of the
pdf
resp. the
pcf
, one wants to make sure that these manipulations do not destroy the fundamental
properties of these objects. Let us consider discrete densities first, and assume that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\in\mathcal{T}
is the discrete representation of a density. Then we should expect

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
, i.e. for each
1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu},\;1\leq\nu\leq d
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}\geq 0
.
We have tacitly assumed that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
is also real-valued; this is not necessarily so
if it is numerically computed with the involvement of the FT. But this condition is easy to
check, as usually the real and imaginary parts of a complex tensor are approximated
separately; one only has to make sure the imaginary part is identical to zero—or
even better, not computed or stored at all by using a half-length compressed real
version of the FFT. This incidentally insures that its FT
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})

is Hermitean. This condition is preserved by the discrete FT.
Coming back to the positivity of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
, this can be checked by
computing its
minimum
component
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}_{\min}
; this minimum should be
non-negative, making
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}
positive.
If it happens to be negative, those components can be removed and
set to zero. It will be explained later in Section
5
on how to do this. Observing this will also
automatically make its FT
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\phi}}}
positive definite.

A further condition is that the density integrates to unity. Hence in the discrete
setting we should expect
\mathcal{S}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})=1
. Incidentally, if
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})
is its FT, then this is equivalent with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}^{0}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Phi}}_{j_{1}^{0},\dots,j_{d}^{0}}=1
.
If
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
does not meet this condition, then it can be rescaled appropriately.

The last task to address in connection with compressed resp. low-rank tensor approximations
is how to compute point-wise functions
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
of a real tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\in\mathcal{T}
,
which means the same function applied to each component.
Normally, in a full representation, this computation is no problem,
but in a compressed representation the component values are not directly accessible.
The Hadamard algebra can be used to accomplish this
function evaluation, continuing
[
33
,
39
]
.
This builds theoretically on generally well known results (e.g.
[
109
]
)
about the evaluation of functions of self-adjoint linear operators like

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}
with the spectral functional calculus, and abstractly
on how to compute functions of self-adjoint elements in an Abelian C*-algebra.
More specifically, as will be seen later, one may use well known
algorithms for (real and self-adjoint) matrices
[
64
]
to actually do the calculations.

The simplest functions like linear combinations follow from the vector space structure of
\mathcal{T}
.
The next in complexity are powers and polynomials. For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the powers are defined as usual by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot m}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot(m-1)}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
, setting
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot 0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
.
This way one may evaluate
polynomials

f_{p}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\beta_{m}t^{m}

by replacing
t^{m}
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot m}
.

The inverse function
f_{i}(t)=1/t=t^{-1}
is easy to deal with, as
the existence of a unit allows the definition of an inverse element:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\in\mathcal{T}

is called invertible, if there exists a—unique—element
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\in\mathcal{T}
such
that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
; it is denoted by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot-1}
.
It is obvious that for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\in\mathcal{T}
to have a Hadamard inverse, no component
can vanish,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{r}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}\neq 0
. and in that case
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot-1}=(1/\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{r}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}})
. This is up to
now only a definition of
f_{i}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}^{\odot-1}
, and not yet an algebraic
way of computing it.

Power series
f_{ps}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\beta_{m}t^{m}
,
or more generally
f_{ps}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\beta_{m}(t-t_{0})^{m}
are—thanks to the Cayley-Hamilton theorem—actually polynomials on the
finite dimensional Hadamard algebra—see the remarks on general functions in the following.
In case
f_{h}
is a holomorphic function in a complex domain containing the
values of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
, it can also be evaluated in the algebra via Cauchy’s formula:

f_{h}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})=\oint_{\Gamma}f_{h}(z)\left(z\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\right)^{\odot-1}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\in\mathcal{T}
,
where
\Gamma
is a contour in
\mathbb{C}
inside the domain of holomorphy,
with all values of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
inside the contour.

The computation of a general function
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})
—where

f
is a real valued function defined on a subset of
\mathbb{R}
which includes all the
values of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}
—makes use of
the representation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}}
from the algebra
(\mathcal{T},\odot)

into the algebra
(\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{T}),\circ)
—the algebra of linear operators with
concatenation “
\circ
” as multiplication. This is an injective algebra homomorphism onto
an Abelian sub-algebra of
\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{T})
of self-adjoint operators.
The general way to compute a function
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})
of a self-adjoint
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}}

is to use the spectral calculus (e.g.
[
109
]
).
This will work in any unital C*-algebra; it is mentioned only
for general background orientation, the situation here is much simpler.
We saw that the operators
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}}
can be represented by diagonal matrices.
This means that
matrix algorithms
, which only use the matrix algebra operations,
can be used to compute
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}})
to represent
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})
,
see e.g.
[
64
]
.
The algorithms will explicitly be addressed later in Section
5
, and may be used
directly on the Hadamard algebra, and there is no need to actually use matrices.
With that in mind, it may be mentioned in passing that as the
spectrum resp. set of eigenvalues of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}}
is a finite set, any function

f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{r}})
could in principle be interpolated by a polynomial. But this is not practical,
as the polynomial would in general have degree
N=\dim\mathcal{T}
, which we assume to be a huge
number, so that typically the matrix algorithms are more economical.

3
Computation of moments and divergences

This section explains how to compute QoIs already mentioned in Section
2

from
pdfs
or
pcfs
given in compressed repesentation.
It is thereby assumed that point-wise functions of the compressed representation resp. low-rank tensor approximation can be computed. Algorithms for this task will be shown
in Section
5
. Moreover, the computation of various
distances and divergences between
pdfs
will be treated in this
discrete setting, noting the point-wise functions which are required to compute them.

If it is desired to compute some QoI—the expected value of a function

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}})
(with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{Y}
) of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
like in
Eq. (
17
), then it is first
necessary to represent this function in low-rank format on the grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}
,
i.e. to find
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})\in\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{T}
.
If
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
represents the
pdf

p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
,
the discrete version of the expectation was already given in
Eq. (
27
):
