\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{G}})={\mathchoice{{{{\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{{$\textstyle V$}}}{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\kern-1.50696pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-1.29167pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\textstyle N$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.01385pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle V$}}}{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle N$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle V$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle N$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle V$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle N$}}}}}}}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{G}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Moments are a special case: for the mean one takes
the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}
, or for higher moments the
tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\otimes k}
:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{1}\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}})
, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k}\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\otimes k})
. For mixed moments these relations can be used in an analogous fashion.

Other characterising functions were sketched at the end of Subsection
2.2
.
The simplest one is the second characteristic function
\chi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\log(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}))

from Eq. (
22
). Given the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}
representing
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
,
this is just the point-wise logarithm

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}=\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}),\quad\text{ such that }\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}=\chi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}).

(31)

From this one may compute cumulants

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k}\approx\left(\mathcal{F}_{d}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\otimes k}\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}})\right)\right)_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}^{0}}.

(32)

The moment generating function
M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathbb{E}\left(\exp\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rangle\right)

Eq. (
23
) can in principle be generated in the same way as
the characteristic function
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rangle)\right)

with the help of the RV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\cdot)

to give its tensor representation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}})
on the grid
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}
.
Its derivatives are directly the moments, so with the FT one has an
alternative approximate computation of the moments:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\,\left(\mathcal{F}_{d}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\right)\right)_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}^{0}},\quad\text{ and }

(33)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k}

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\otimes k})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}^{k}\,\left(\mathcal{F}_{d}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\otimes k}\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\right)\right)_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}^{0}}.

(34)

Similarly to the discrete version of second characteristic function Eq. (
31
),
the cumulant generating function
K_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\log(M_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}))
in Eq. (
25
)
also has a discrete version, the point-wise logarithm of the discrete moment generating
function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}=\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}),\quad\text{ such that }\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}=K_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{T}}}).

(35)

Analogous as before in Eq. (
34
), the cumulants may be expressed via

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k}\approx\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}^{k}\,\left(\mathcal{F}_{d}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\otimes k}\odot\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}})\right)\right)_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{j}}^{0}}.

(36)

If the discretised
pcf

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}
has
low tensor rank, then
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{k}
will also be of a low-rank.
Note that we cannot say the same about
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k}
from Eq. (
32
)
(or Eq. (
36
)). The tensor rank of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{k}
depends on the tensor rank
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}
(or
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}
), and the latter can have a high rank due to the
involved
\log(\cdot)
function. Hence it is assumed here that the series expansion
described in Subsection
5.4
will allow it to approximate
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Upsilon}}

(or
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Z}}
) in a low-rank tensor format.

Table 1:
List of some typical divergences and distances.

D_{\text{KL}}
:

{\displaystyle\int\left(\log(p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})/q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}))\right)p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathbb{E}_{p}(\log(p/q))}

(D_{H})^{2}
:

{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\left(\sqrt{p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})}-\sqrt{q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}}

Bregman divergence —
D_{\phi}
:

{\displaystyle\int\left[(\phi(p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}))-\phi(q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})))-(p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})-q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}))\phi^{\prime}(q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}))\right]\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}}

Bhattacharyya distance —
D_{\text{Bh}}
:

{\displaystyle-\log\left(\int\sqrt{(p(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})q(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}))}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\right)}

Let us now turn to quantities which characterise the whole distribution, or
the difference or distance between them.
A simple characterisation of a distribution is its entropy, which was
already encountered in Section
1
. For a continuous distribution

p
this is the
differential entropy
, requiring the
point-wise logarithm of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
:

h(p):=\mathbb{E}_{p}(-\log(p))\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(-\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}))=-\frac{V}{N}\left\langle\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\right\rangle,

(37)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\cdot)
is computed as in Eq. (
27
).

Table 2:
Discrete approximations for divergences listed in Table
1
. Formulas for computations are given in Section
2
and Section
5
.

Name of the divergence

Approximation of
D_{\bullet}(p\|q)

Kullback–Leibler-(KL) —
D_{\text{KL}}
:

{\displaystyle\frac{V}{N}(\left\langle\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}})\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\right\rangle)}

squared Hellinger distance —
(D_{H})^{2}
:

{\displaystyle\frac{V}{2N}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}^{\odot 1/2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\odot 1/2}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}^{\odot 1/2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\odot 1/2}\right\rangle}

Bregman divergence —
D_{\phi}
:

{\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\left((\phi(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})-\phi(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}))-(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}})\odot\phi^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}})\right)}

Bhattacharyya distance —
D_{\text{Bh}}
:

{\displaystyle-\log\left(\frac{V}{N}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}^{\odot 1/2}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\odot 1/2}\right\rangle\right)}

To compare two
pdfs

p
and
q
, one uses divergences or
distances between them. The best known is probably the relative entropy, also known as
the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence.
Divergences are a kind of generalisation
of distances, but unlike distances they do not have to be symmetric.
Let
p,q\in\mathfrak{D}
be two
pdfs
, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathcal{T}

their low-rank tensor representations.
Some well known divergences and distances are given
in Table
1
.
For the Bregman divergence,
\phi
has to be a real convex function,
e.g.
\phi(t)=t^{2}
. These divergences and distances may be computed by
the discrete approximations shown in Table
2
.

It is evident that one has to be able to compute the point-wise logarithm and
the square root of a low-rank tensor.
For the Bregmann divergence one has to be able to compute point-wise the
convex function
\phi
and its derivative
\phi^{\prime}
of a low-rank tensor
representation, an easy task for
\phi(t)=t^{2}
.

The
f
-divergence is a way to define many divergences in a unifying way

[
82
,
90
]
, depending on a convex function
f
.
One requires that
f
be a convex function,
satisfying
f(1)=0
. Then
the
f
-divergence of
p
from
q
and its discrete approximation is defined as

D_{f}(p\|q):={\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{q}\left(f\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)\right)}\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}}\left(f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\odot-1})\right)=\frac{V}{N}\left\langle f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\odot-1})\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\right\rangle.

(38)

Many common divergences, such as the KL-divergence, the Hellinger distance, and the
total variation distance, are special cases of the
f
-divergence, coinciding
with a particular choice of
f
. In Table
3
the functions
f
for
some common
f
-divergences
[
82
,
90
]
are listed.

Table 3:
Some examples of the function
f
for the
f
-divergence.

Name of the divergence

Corresponding
f(t)

KL-divergence

{\displaystyle t\log(t)}

reverse KL-divergence

{\displaystyle-\log(t)}

squared Hellinger distance

{\displaystyle({\sqrt{t}}-1)^{2}}

total variation distance

{\displaystyle\left|t-1\right|/2}

Pearson
\chi^{2}_{P}
-divergence

{\displaystyle(t-1)^{2}}

Neyman
\chi^{2}_{N}
-divergence (reverse Pearson)

{\displaystyle t^{-1}-1}

Pearson-Vajda
\chi^{k}_{P}
-divergence

{\displaystyle(t-1)^{k}}

Pearson-Vajda
\left|\chi\right|^{k}_{P}
-divergence

{\displaystyle\left|t-1\right|^{k}}

Jensen-Shannon-divergence

{\displaystyle t\log(t)-(t+1)\log((t+1)/2)}

Hence to compute the discrete approximation, one has to be able to compute not
only the Hadamard inverse of a low rank tensor, but also the function
f
.
These are essentially the ones which have been discussed already, the only new
one is the absolute value.

4
Tensor formats

In this section some well-known technical details scattered in the literature
about algebraic computations in low-rank tensor formats are collected.
Many tensor formats are used in quantum physics under the name
tensor networks
,
see
[
118
,
105
,
41
,
93
,
18
,
14
]
.
The canonical polyadic (CP)
[
65
]
and
Tucker

[
114
]
formats
have been well known for a long time and are therefore very
popular. For instance, CP and Tucker rank-structured tensor formats have been
applied in chemometrics and in signal processing
[
111
,
21
]
.
The
Tensor Train
format was originally developed in quantum physics and
chemistry as “matrix product states” (MPS), see
[
118
]
and references therein,
and rediscovered and developed further in
[
99
,
100
,
7
,
26
]
as tensor train format.
The hierarchical tensor (HT) format was introduced in
[
58
]
, and further
considered in
[
51
]
.

The CP format is cheap, it is simpler than the Tucker or TT format, but,
compared to others, there are no reliable algorithms to compute CP decompositions
for
d>2

[
59
,
75
]
.
The Tucker format has stable algorithms
[
74
]
,
but the storage and complexity costs are

\mathcal{O}(d\,r\,n+r^{d})
, i.e. they grow exponentially with
d
.
The TT format is a bit more complicated, but does not have this disadvantage. Applications to UQ problems are described in
[
24
,
25
,
23
]
.

The higher the order of the tensor, the more possibilities there are to find
a low-rank approximation
[
59
]
, as such
tensors contain not only rows and columns, but also

slices
and
fibres

[
77
,
78
,
22
,
59
]
.
These can be analysed for linear dependencies, super symmetry,
or sparsity, and may result in a strong data compression
[
115
]
.

4.1
The canonical polyadic (CP) tensor format

The CP representation of a multivariate function is one of the easiest tensor representations,
it was developed in
[
65
]
.
A tensor representation is a multi-linear mapping. There are many different representations, e.g. the
CP tensor representation maps a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
into a sum with
r
terms

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{i,\nu}
. The number
r
is called the tensor rank.
The storage required to store a tensor in the CP tensor format is
\mathcal{O}(r\,d\,n)

(assuming that
n=n_{1}=\ldots=n_{d}
).
Denoting the set of all rank-
r
tensors by
\mathcal{T}^{r}
, it is evident that if

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{r}
, then the sum
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{1}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{2}\notin\mathcal{T}^{r}
,
and, therefore,
\mathcal{T}^{r}
is not a vector space
[
40
]
.
This sum is generally in
\mathcal{T}^{2r}
.

A complete description of fundamental operations in the canonical
tensor format and their numerical cost can be found in

[
59
]
. For recent algorithms in the canonical tensor format we
refer to
[
55
,
12
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.
To multiply a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
by a scalar
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
costs
\mathcal{O}(r\,n\,d)
:

\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\alpha\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{j,\nu}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}{\alpha_{\nu}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{j,\nu},

(39)

where
\alpha_{\nu}:=\sqrt[d]{|\alpha|}
for all
\nu>1
,
and
\alpha_{1}:=\sign(\alpha)\sqrt[d]{|\alpha|}
.

The sum of two tensors costs
only
\mathcal{O}(1)
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r_{u}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j,\nu}\right)+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r_{v}}\bigotimes_{\mu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{k,\mu}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{u}+r_{v}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{j,\nu},

(40)

where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{j,\nu}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j,\nu}
for
j\leq r_{u}
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{j,\nu}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{j,\nu}
for
r_{u}<j\leq r_{u}+r_{v}
.
The sum
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
generally has rank
r_{u}+r_{v}
.
The Hadamard product can be written as follows

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r_{u}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j,\nu}\right)\odot\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r_{v}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{k,\nu}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{u}}\sum_{k=1}^{r_{v}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j,\nu}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{k,\nu}\right).

(41)

The new rank can increase till
r_{u}r_{v}
, and the computational cost is

\mathcal{O}(r_{u}\,r_{v}n\,d)
.
The scalar product can be computed as follows:

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}=\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{r_{u}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j,\nu}\mid\sum_{k=1}^{r_{v}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{k,\nu}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{u}}\sum_{k=1}^{r_{v}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j,\nu}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{k,\nu}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{P}_{\nu}}.

(42)

The computational cost is
\mathcal{O}(r_{u}\,r_{v}\,n\,d)
.
The tensor rank can be truncated via the ALS-method or Gauss-Newton-method
[
35
,
40
]
. The scalar product above helps to compute the Frobenius norm
\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\|_{2}:=\sqrt{\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}}
.

4.2
The Tensor Train format

The tensor train (TT) format is described in
[
97
,
100
,
59
,
75
]
.
As already noted, it was originally developed in quantum chemistry as “matrix product
states” (MPS), see
[
118
]
and references therein, and rediscovered later

[
99
,
100
]
.

Definition 4.1

(TT-Format, TT-Representation, TT-Ranks)
.

The
TT-tensor format
is for variable
TT-representation
ranks

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=(r_{0},\dots,r_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
— with
r_{0}=r_{d}=1
and
under the assumption that
d>2
— defined by the
following multilinear mapping

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{U}}_{\mathrm{TT}}:\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{TT},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}}

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\raise-2.20001pt\hbox{\huge$\times$}}{\raise-0.50003pt\hbox{\Large$\times$}}{\hbox{\large$\times$}}{\times}}_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathcal{P}_{\nu}^{r_{\nu-1}\times r_{\nu}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T},\quad\mathcal{P}_{\nu}=\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}\;(\nu=1,\dots,d),

(43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{TT},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}

\displaystyle=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}^{(\nu)}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}})\in\mathcal{P}_{\nu}^{r_{\nu-1}\times r_{\nu}}:1\leq j_{\nu}\leq r_{\nu},1\leq\nu\leq d)

\displaystyle\qquad\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{U}}_{\mathrm{TT}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\sum_{j_{0}=1}^{r_{0}}\dots\sum_{j_{d}=1}^{r_{d}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}\;\in\mathcal{T}.

We call
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}:=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=U_{\mathrm{TT},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})
a tensor
represented in the train tensor format.
Note that the TT-cores
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}
may be viewed as a vector
valued
r_{\nu-1}\times r_{\nu}
matrix
with the vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{w}}^{(\nu)}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
with the components

w^{(\nu)}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}[i_{\nu}]:1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu}

at index position
(j_{\nu-1},j_{\nu})
. The representation in components is then

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\sum_{j_{0}=1}^{r_{0}}\dots\sum_{j_{d}=1}^{r_{d}}w_{j_{0}j_{1}}^{(1)}[i_{1}]\cdots w_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}[i_{\nu}]\cdots w_{j_{d-1}j_{d}}^{(d)}[i_{d}]

(44)

Alternatively, each TT-core
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}
may be seen as a vector
of
r_{\nu-1}\times r_{\nu}
matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
of length
M_{\nu}
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}):1\leq i_{\nu}\leq M_{\nu}
. Then
the representation Eq. (
44
) reads

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}},

(45)

which explains the name
matrix product state
. Observe that the first matrix
is always a row vector as
r_{0}=1
, and the last matrix is always a column vector as
r_{d}=1
.
The matrix components
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
of the TT-cores are also called “carriages”
or “waggons” with “wheels”
i_{\nu}
at the bottom, coupled to the next “carriage”
or “waggon” via the matrix product. This explains the
tensor train
name.
If one notes more carefully
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nu}^{r_{\nu-1}\times r_{\nu}}=\mathbb{R}^{r_{\nu-1}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{r_{\nu}}
, then Eq. (
45
)
can be written more concisely as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{U}}_{\mathrm{TT}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(1)}\times_{3}^{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(2)}\times_{3}^{1}\cdots\times_{3}^{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(d)},

(46)

where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\times_{k}^{\ell}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
is a contraction of the
k
-th index of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}

with the
\ell
-th index of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
, where one often writes just
\times_{k}
for

\times^{1}_{k}
. Thus in Eq. (
46
) the contractions leave the indices from
the
\mathbb{R}^{M_{\nu}}
untouched, so that the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
is formed.

Each TT-core (or
block
)
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}
is defined by

r_{\nu-1}\times r_{\nu}\times M_{\nu}
numbers. Assuming
n=M_{\nu}
for all
\nu=1,\ldots,d
,
the total number of entries scales as
\mathcal{O}(d\,n\,r^{2})
, which is tractable
as long as
r=\max\{r_{k}\}
is moderate.

We follow to the work of Oseledets
[
100
]
and list the major properties of the
TT-tensor format.

The multiplication with scalar

\alpha
could be simply
done by multiplying one of the TT-cores
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}
in the
representation Eq. (
46
) for any
\nu
in

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(1)}\times_{3}^{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(2)}\times_{3}^{1}\cdots\times_{3}^{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(d)}
.
But to balance the effect better, define
\alpha_{\nu}:=\sqrt[d]{|\alpha|}
for all
\nu>1
,
and
\alpha_{1}:=\sign(\alpha)\sqrt[d]{|\alpha|}
. Then
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}=\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}

is given by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{w}}}=(\alpha_{1}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(1)})\times_{3}^{1}(\alpha_{2}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(2)})\times_{3}^{1}\cdots\times_{3}^{1}(\alpha_{d}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(d)})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}^{(1)}\times_{3}^{1}\cdots\times_{3}^{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}^{(d)}.

The new cores are given by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{W}}}^{(\nu)}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{W}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{W}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{W}}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}})=(\alpha_{\nu}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}})
, a sequence of new “carriage” matrices.
The computational complexity is
\mathcal{O}(d\,n\,r^{2})
.

Addition of two TT-tensors

Assume two tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
are given in the TT-tensor format
as in Eq. (
45
), i.e.
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{u}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
and

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
. The sum

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
is given by the new cores
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
such that

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
, where

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}\end{pmatrix},\quad 1\leq i_{\nu}\leq r_{\nu},2\leq\nu\leq d-1.

and the first and the last cores will be

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}_{i_{1}}=\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(d)}_{i_{d}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}^{(d)}_{i_{d}}\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}^{(d)}_{i_{d}}\end{array}\right).

As only storage may have to be concatenated, the computational cost is
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
but as the carriages resp. TT-cores grow, the final rank will generally be the
sum of the ranks.

The Hadamard product

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
in the TT format
is computed as follows. Assume two tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
are given in
the TT tensor format as in Eq. (
45
), i.e.
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{u}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
and

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}
. The Hadamard product is

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{w}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}})=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{u}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{d}}).

The tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
has also the TT-tensor format, namely with the new cores

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{U}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}\otimes_{\mathrm{K}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}},\quad 1\leq i_{\nu}\leq r_{\nu},1\leq\nu\leq d,

where
\otimes_{\mathrm{K}}
is the Kronecker product of two matrices
[
59
]
.
The rank of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{(\nu)}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}})
is the product
of the ranks of the TT-cores
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{(\nu)}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{(\nu)}
.

The Euclidean inner product

of two tensors in the TT-format
as in Eq. (
43
)

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}=\sum_{j_{0}=1}^{r^{u}_{0}}\dots\sum_{j_{d}=1}^{r^{u}_{d}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}^{(\nu)},\qquad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}=\sum_{j_{0}=1}^{r^{v}_{0}}\dots\sum_{j_{d}=1}^{r^{v}_{d}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}^{(\nu)},

with ranks
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}^{u}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}^{v}
can be computed as follows:

\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{T}}=\sum_{j_{0}=1}^{r^{u}_{0}}\dots\sum_{j_{d}=1}^{r^{u}_{d}}\sum_{i_{0}=1}^{r^{v}_{0}}\dots\sum_{i_{d}=1}^{r^{v}_{d}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{d}\;\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}_{j_{\nu-1}j_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{v}}_{i_{\nu-1}i_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{P}_{\nu}}.

The computational complexity is
\mathcal{O}(d\,n\,r^{4})
, and can be reduced further
[
98
]
.

Rank truncation in the TT format.

The rank truncation operation is based on the SVD algorithm and requires

\mathcal{O}(d\,n\,r^{3})
operations
[
50
]
. The TT-rounding algorithm (p. 2305 in
[
100
]
) is based on QR decomposition and costs
\mathcal{O}(d\,n\,r^{3})
.

Corollary 2.4 in
[
100
]
states that for a given tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
and
rank bounds
r_{k}
, the best approximation to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
in the Frobenius norm with TT-ranks
bounded by
r_{k}
always exist (denote it by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{*}
), and the TT-approximation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}
computed by the TT-SVD algorithm (p. 2301 in
[
100
]
) is quasi-optimal:

\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{d-1}\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{*}\|_{F}.

(47)

In
[
79
]
the authors suggested a new re-compression
randomised algorithm for Tucker and TT tensor formats.
For the rank-adaptive DMRG-cross algorithm see
[
107
]
, and its extension in
[
32
]
.

5
Algorithms

As one may have gleaned from the preceding,
very often not only the density
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
is of interest, but
expected values of functions of the density,
e.g. in order to compute an
f
-divergence or the entropy.
This section lists well-known numerical algorithms to actually compute functions
from Table
3
like

f(p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}))
by
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})
, where the
f
’s considered are

f(\cdot)=\{\sign(\cdot),(\cdot)^{-1},\sqrt{\cdot},\sqrt[m]{\cdot},(\cdot)^{k},\log{(\cdot)},\exp{(\cdot)},(\cdot)^{2},|\cdot|\},

(48)

k>0
, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
is a tensor which represents the values of
p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
on a
discretisation grid as explained in Subsection
2.3
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}=p_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{X}}})=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{p}}\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{j,\nu}
.
The
m
-th root is needed for scaling.

Here we collect iterative algorithms and series expansions which have appeared in various places
scattered in the literature, and which are used to compute functions in matrix algebras.
As was shown in Section
2
, these can also be used in the Hadamard algebra,
which is isomorphic to an algebra of diagonal matrices.
These algorithms are non-trivial and their detailed discussion is beyond the scope of
this work, for this we point to the relevant literature. Specific possible difficulties which may
appear if they are used in the low-rank representaion are:
the intermediate tensor ranks of iterates may become very large, or a stable rank truncation
procedure may either not be available or it may be very computationally intensive.
Included are also algorithms specific to low-rank tensor representations,
the so-called
cross-approximation
, see Subsection
5.5
.

5.1
Consistency of data

Making sure the data one works with is consistent is of great importance, and
should be checked before any other computations. This was already touched upon at the
end of Subsection
2.3
. Recall that for the continuous case, the
pdf
has to
be in the set
\mathfrak{D}
(cf. Eq. (
16
)), and the
pcf
has to be in
the set
\mathfrak{C}
(cf. Eq. (
19
)). The discussion at the
end of Subsection
2.3
also showed that these conditions are most easily checked
by making sure that the discretised
pdf
(cf. Eq. (
2
))
is real valued and positive
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
, and that the discrete integral
Eq. (
26
) evaluates to unity,
\mathcal{S}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})=1
.

That the
pdf
is real valued is translated by the Fourier transform (FT)
into the
pcf
being Hermitean. The discrete FT preserves these
properties, and these properties are preserved also
in the low-rank representation. To check that the discretised low-rank representation of the
pdf
is real valued,
one only has to make sure that the imaginary part vanishes—it does not even
have to be represented.

The condition that the
pdf
is non-negative, which the Fourier transform (FT)
translates into the
pcf
being positive definite, is preserved also by the discrete FT.
But this is a local, point-wise condition, and it may be violated in the process
of compression or low-rank approximation, and is also not so easily checked in
a compressed low-rank format.
It was already stated in Subsection
2.3
that this is equivalent to the
positivity of the convolution operator
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}}
using the
pcf
tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Phi}}
, whose spectrum are the values of the
pdf
tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
,
but this is not easily checked either. As will be shown later in Subsection
5.3
,
it is possible to compute the level set function
\Lambda_{U}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})
Eq. (
64
)
for the level set
U=]-\infty,0]
. In case this happens to be non-zero, indicating some indices

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}
where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{P}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}\geq 0
may be violated, one obtains a corrected

pdf
tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{c}
by seting

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{c}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}-\Lambda_{U}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}).

(49)

This new approximation has all the faulty negative values removed, so that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{c}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
. This change will not affect the Hermitean or self-adjoint
character discussed earlier.

Through a correction like Eq. (
49
), or just due to the the process
of compression or low-rank approximation, the condition
\mathcal{S}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})=1=\beta
may not be
satisfied any more. In case this is not satisfied, say
1\neq\beta>0
,
for a re-scaled
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{s}
one may set

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{s}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}/\beta.

(50)

This re-scaling will not affect the positive character of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
.

5.2
Overview of methods to compute tensor functions

The topic of point-wise functions was already discussed at the
end of Subsection
2.3
.
There are many methods to compute
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
. Most of these only use the fact that the
underlying structure (here of
\mathcal{T}
) is that of a unital C*-algebra
[
109
]
,
and—at least implicitly—define the functions via spectral calculus.
Below we have collected and adapted some of the classical matrix algorithms
for computing the tensor functions listed above in Eq. (
48
).

Many algorithms developed (cf.
[
64
]
) to compute functions of matrices use
only operations from the underlying algebra. They can thus be used in any unital C*-algebra.
Exponential sums to
compute
{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}^{\odot-1}
are implemented
in
[
17
,
13
]
. Some other methods and estimates are developed
in
[
49
,
60
,
71
]
.
The function
{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}^{\odot-1/2}
was
computed in
[
75
,
71
]
, and

{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}^{\odot-\mu}
in
[
61
,
71
]
.
A quadrature rule to compute the Dunford-Cauchy contour integral for holomorphic fucntions
mentioned at the end of Subsection
2.3

is presented in
[
42
,
43
,
56
,
57
,
83
,
75
]
.
Iterative methods of Newton and Newton-Schultz type are used in

[
35
,
40
,
34
,
11
,
33
]
.
The inversion
{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}^{\odot-1}
in the Tucker format is done by the
Newton–Schultz approximate iteration in
[
95
]
.

The various TT-cross approximation algorithms
[
99
,
7
,
26
,
25
]

are a well-known alternative to iterative methods and series expansions, and they are sketched later
in Subsection
5.5
.
Whereas the previously mentioned algorithms work in any unital C
∗
-algebra, the cross approximation
algorithms make use of a specific representation. We focus here on the tensor train (TT) representation.
The TT-cross approximation computes a low-rank TT approximation of
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
for a given
function
f(\cdot)
and a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
directly, “on the fly”.
The TT-cross algorithms assume that the full tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}:=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
is not given explicitly,
but rather as a function which can return any element
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}}:=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}})

for a given index
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}
.
The Tucker cross algorithm to approximate
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1}

was used in
[
94
]
. In
[
116
]
, the multigrid Cross 3D
algorithm is used to calculate the Gauss polynomial. In
[
96
]
,
iterative methods in the Tucker format are used to compute
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot 1/3}
. In
[
7
,
36
]
, the authors are
using the cross method for the Hierarchical
Tucker format to estimate various functionals of the solution in the UQ context.
In
[
63
,
83
]
the sinc quadrature is used to compute fractional
derivatives of the
d
-dimensional Laplace operator.
The TT-approximation of the sign function was computed in

[
24
,
23
]
, but the TT-ranks were large.
The point-wise inverse, level sets, and sign functions are defined and computed in
[
33
]
.
The point-wise inverse is required for computing the point-wise functions

\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
and
\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
,
[
33
]
. The Newton algorithm for
computing
\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
and
\exp(-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
, its convergence and error analysis for
hierarchical matrices were considered in
[
52
]
.

Polynomials are certainly the simplest functions, as they are directly elements of the algebra.
General polynomials one would numerically
compute by e.g. using Horner’s scheme in order to minimise the number
of Hadamard products. Here only simple powers will be needed
as an auxiliary function for a kind of “scaling”,
namely iterated squaring or iterated inverse squaring.
In some of the algorithms the scaling factor involves e.g.
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}
,
we shall sketch at the end of the following Subsection
5.3
how to compute that.

Denoting the power function by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot m}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
,
one also wants to use it for negative powers; for
m<0
this is simply

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(-m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1})
,
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1}
is shown in Subsection
5.3
.
A really simple and well known way to
make sure that no unnecessary multiplications are performed is for
m\in\mathbb{N}
given by the recursive
formula (although usually implemented in a loop):

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\begin{cases}m>1\text{ and odd}:&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(m-1,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}});\\
m\text{ even}:&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}({\mathchoice{{{{\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\kern-1.50696pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-1.29167pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.01385pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}({\mathchoice{{{{\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\kern-1.50696pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-1.29167pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.01385pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}});\\
m=1:&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}};\end{cases}

(51)

Remark 5.1
.

While performing algebraic operations on tensors in some compressed format, the tensor ranks are increasing. To keep computational cost low, a tensor compression
after each or after a number of algebraic operations
may be necessary.
We perform the
truncation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{T}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}_{\epsilon}
to
low rank

r
with error
\epsilon

[
59
,
11
]
.
We thus allow that the algebraic operations are possibly
only executed approximately. It is assumed that such an approximation is performed whenever necessary,
and it will not be always pointed out explicitly.

5.3
Iterative methods

We want to compute
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
for some function
f:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}
from the list at the beginning of this section. We describe how to do it through iteration (see also
[
39
]
). Thus we have an iteration function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
,
which only uses operations from the Hadamard algebra on
\mathcal{T}
, and which is iterated,

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{i+1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{i})
, and converges to a fixed point
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}
.
When started with a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}
depending on
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
, the fixed point is
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{i}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*})=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
.
A Newton-type family of high-order iterative methods for some matrix functions
was discussed in
[
3
]
.

To deal with truncation in iterative algorithms,
the standard iteration map

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
is replaced by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{T}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}_{\epsilon}\circ\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
.
Here one speaks about a
perturbed or truncated iteration

[
62
,
89
,
39
]
.
The general structure of the iterative
algorithms for a post-processing task
f

or for an auxiliary function is shown in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Iteration with truncation

1:

Input: tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}
; output: tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{n}
after
n
iterations

2:

Start with some initial
compressed
guess
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
.

3:

i\leftarrow 0

4:

while

no convergence

do

5:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{i+1}\leftarrow\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{T}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}_{\epsilon}\circ\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{i})

6:

i\leftarrow i+1

7:

end

while

In case the iteration by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
is
super-linearly

convergent, the
truncated iteration
