\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{T}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}_{\epsilon}\circ\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
will
still
converge
super-linearly, but finally
stagnate
in an

\epsilon
-neighbourhood of the fixed point
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}

[
62
]
.
If the iteration by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
is
linearly
convergent with
contraction

factor
q
, the
truncated iteration

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{T}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{T}}_{\epsilon}\circ\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{f}
will
still
converge
linearly, but finally

stagnate
in an
\epsilon/(1-q)
-neighbourhood
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}

[
89
]
. Ideas how to choose the starting value
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}
are given in
[
39
]
.

Computing the point-wise inverse
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1}
.

Apply Newton’s method to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\odot-1}
to
approximate the inverse of a given tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
, and one obtains
[
95
]
the
following iteration function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\odot-1}
—to be used with Algorithm
1

with the initial iterate
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}

to bring
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}
close to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{a}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\odot-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\odot(2\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}−\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}).

(52)

The iteration converges if the initial iterate
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}

satisfies
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}−\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}\right\|_{\infty}<1
.
A possible candidate for the starting value is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
with

\alpha<(1/\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty})^{2}
.
For such a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}
, the convergence initial
condition
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot 2}\right\|_{\infty}<1
is always satisfied.
As the initial iterate was scaled, the fixed point of the iteration is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}=(1/\alpha)\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1}
, and
thus the final result is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1}=\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}
.

We did not assume that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
is invertible, as the iteration actually computes the pseudo-inverse
(i.e. only the non-zero entries are inverted). It is easily seen from Eq. (
52
) that
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
zero entries in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
stay zero during the iteration.

Computing point-wise
\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
resp.
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})^{\odot{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}}}
.

The Newton iteration for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\odot 2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}

uses the iteration function Eq. (
53
)
together with Algorithm
1
.

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\sqrt{}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{\odot-1}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}).

(53)

The starting value can be
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}})/2
, other stating values
obtained through scaling are described later.

Unfortunately, Eq. (
53
) contains an inverse power
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{\odot-1}
and can thus
not be computed directly only with operations from the algebra. One could use the just described
algorithm for the inverse, but the nested iteration is usually not so advantageous.
An alternative is the well known stable inversion free Newton-Schulz iteration
[
64
]
,
which simultaneously computes
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{+}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}}}

and its inverse
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{-}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}}}
.
Setting
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}_{0}]=[\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}]\in\mathcal{T}^{2}
,
the iteration function is
best written using the auxiliary function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}})=3\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\sqrt{}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}})\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}})\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\end{bmatrix}.

(54)

The iteration converges to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{*}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{+},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{-}]=[\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}_{0}},(\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}_{0}})^{\odot-1}]

if
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}_{0}\right\|_{\infty}<1
, which can be achieved with a scaling factor
\alpha<1/\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}
.
As the initial iterate was scaled, the fixed point of the iteration is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{+}=\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{-}=(1/\sqrt{\alpha})\cdot(\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}})^{\odot-1}
. Thus the final result is

\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}=(1/\sqrt{\alpha})\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{+}
and

(\sqrt{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}})^{\odot-1}=\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{*}^{-}
.

Computing
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}}
when
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

This function may be needed for logarithmic scaling purposes, see Subsection
5.4
.
For the theory and required definitions see Section 7 in
[
64
]
.
A new family of high-order iterative methods for the matrix
m
-th root was suggested in
[
4
]
.
To compute the principal
m
-th root of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
,
where it is assumed that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
, one considers
Newton’s method for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\odot m}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
.
The iteration function with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}

corresponding to Eq. (
53
) looks like

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{m-\text{root}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})=\frac{1}{m}\left((m-1)\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(1-m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}\right).

(55)

If
m\geq 2
, this involves a negative power
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{\odot(1-m)}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(1-m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})
.
The convergence analysis is rather complicated, see more in Section 7.3,
[
64
]
, but the algorithm converges for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
.

Just as there is a more stable version Eq. (
54
) for
m=2
avoiding inverses, so one has a “double iteration”
[
64
]
here as well.
It is best written using the auxiliary function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}})=(1/m)\cdot((m+1)\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}})
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{m-\text{root}}=\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}})\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{A}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}))\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\end{bmatrix},

(56)

where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}_{i}\to\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}_{i}\to\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}}
.
The starting values are
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}_{0}]=[\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}},(\alpha)^{m}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}]\in\mathcal{T}^{2}
, with

\alpha<(\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}/\sqrt{2})^{-{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}}
.
For scaling purposes it is best used with
m=2^{k}
.

Another way of computing the
m
-th root is Tsai’s algorithm
[
113
,
85
]
,
which uses the auxiliary function

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{B}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}})=(2\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}+(m-2)\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}})\odot(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}+(m-1)\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}})^{\odot-1}
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{Tsai}=\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\text{pow}}(m,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{B}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}))\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\odot(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{B}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}))\end{bmatrix},

(57)

with starting value
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}]
. Then
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}_{i}\to\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}}
.

Computing
\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
.

The tensor
\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})\in\mathcal{T}
is defined point-wise for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}\in\mathcal{I}
by

(\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}))_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}:=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}1,&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}>0;\\
-1,&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}<0;\\
\phantom{-}0,&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}=0.\end{cases}

(58)

The equation to be used for the Newton iteration is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{F}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{F}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
. With starting value
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}

one obtains the following iteration function:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{\sign}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})=\frac{1}{2}(\mu\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}+\frac{1}{\mu}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{\odot-1}),

(59)

with
\mu=\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{\odot-1}\right\|_{\infty}/\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\right\|_{\infty}
(cf. Section 8.6 in
[
64
]
).
This method converges to
\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
.

Alternatively, one can rewrite this iteration function without computing
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}^{\odot-1}
, namely

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{\Psi}}_{NS}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\odot(3\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}),

(60)

with starting value
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{0}=\alpha\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
,
where
\alpha=\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot-1}\right\|_{\infty}/\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}
.
The last formula is called the Newton-Schulz iteration, it has quadratic (local) convergence to

\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})

[
35
,
33
,
38
,
64
]
.

Computing the absolute value
\left|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right|

is simple if
\sign(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})

Eq. (
58
) is available (see above):
Having the
\sign(\cdot)
function, one can compute the absolute value,
characteristic function of a set, and the level set function:

\left|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right|=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\sign{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})}.

(61)

Computing the characteristic function of a sub-set

of values
I\subset\mathbb{R}

of a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
can be done by using the shifted
\sign
-function Eq. (
58
)
[
37
,
38
,
39
]
.
The
characteristic
function of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\in\mathcal{T}
in an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a tensor
\chi_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})\in\mathcal{T}
.
It is defined for every multi-index
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}
point-wise as

(\chi_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}))_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}:=\begin{cases}1,&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}\in I;\\
0,&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{i}}}\notin I.\end{cases}

(62)

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. If
I=(-\infty,b)
, then

\chi_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\frac{1}{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}+\sign(b\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}))
. If
I=(a,+\infty)
,
then
\chi_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\frac{1}{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\sign(a\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}))
.
And if
I=[a,b]
, then

\chi_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\frac{1}{2}(\sign(b\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})-\sign(a\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})).

(63)

Computing the level set function of a sub-set

of values
I\subset\mathbb{R}
of a tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
may be accomplished using the characteristic function Eq. (
63
)

[
37
,
38
,
39
]
:

\Lambda_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\chi_{I}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}.

(64)

Computing
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}=\max\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\varrho(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
and

\min\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
.

We need these values in almost every algorithm above and for checking the consistency. For instance, if

\min\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
then
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\geq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}
. It was already pointed out that

\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}=\max\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=\varrho(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
is the largest—by magnitude—eigenvalue of the associated linear
operator
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\odot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
, and thus itertive eigenvalue algorithms
can be used to compute it. One of the simplest is the
power iteration. It can be modified in this special case here to greatly increase its convergence speed,
essentially by repeated squaring.
The modified power iteration algorithm is described in

[
53
,
39
]
. The
convergence is
exponential
with the rate
|\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}|^{2^{i}}
, where
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are the largest and the second largest eigenvalues of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
.

To compute
\min\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
(the smallest eigenvalue) or some intermediate eigenvalues of

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{L}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}}
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
we suggest to use
the well-known shifting or inverse shifting functions
[
33
,
39
]
.
These and similar techniques are well known from eigenvalue
calculations of large / sparse symmetric matrices

[
45
,
102
,
104
,
119
]
.

5.4
Series expansions

Computing
\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
.

We assume that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}>0
.
Various algorithms, improvement ideas, stability issues and tricks to compute

\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
are discussed in
[
20
]
.
Improved inverse scaling and squaring algorithms for the matrix logarithm were suggested
later in
[
2
]
. We suggest to follow these works for the case when
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
is a tensor.
The stability of the matrix arithmetic-geometric mean iterations for computing the matrix
logarithm is investigated in
[
19
]
.
For the algorithms to work well
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
has to be close to the identity
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
,
which can be achieved by taking roots:
for
\lambda>0
one has
\log\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\lambda}\right)=\lambda\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
.

From matrix calculus it is known
[
20
,
2
]
that one of the ways
to compute
\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
close to the identity
is to truncate the Taylor series (radius of convergence
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}\right\|_{\infty}<1
):

\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}})=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\odot n}

where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
. If
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
is not near to the identity,
then one may use the relation
\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=2^{k}\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot 1/2^{k}})
,
where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot 1/2^{k}}\to\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}
as
k
increases
[
69
]
.

Another way to compute the logarithm of a positive

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
is Gregory’s series
[
64
]
, which converges for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}>0
.
Setting
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})\odot(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{1}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})^{\odot-1}
, one has

\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}=-2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2k+1}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}^{\odot(2k+1)}.

(65)

Obviously,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}
involves an inverse, but it has to be computed only once.
We note that the tensor ranks (according to Section
4.2
) in
Eq. (
65
) may increase very fast.

Computing
\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
.

One of the standard algorithms using power series together with scaling and squaring is explained in
[
64
]
(Chapter 10):

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}_{r,s}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{r}\frac{1}{k!s^{k}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}^{\odot k}\right)^{\odot s}.

(66)

Here
\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}_{r,s}=\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{u}}_{r,s}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
.
It is of advantage to use
s
from the series of powers of
2
,
s=1,2,4,\dots,2^{k}
, then the
s
-th power can be computed by squaring. For the scaling the best choice is
\alpha>\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}\right\|_{\infty}
.

5.5
Direct approximation by TT-cross-algorithms

In this section, we discuss the so-called cross algorithms
[
99
,
7
,
26
,
25
]
for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}:=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
.
Some extensions of the TT-Cross and ALS-Cross algorithms (e.g. the AMEn algorithm) were suggested by
S. Dolgov and co-authors, and can be found in
[
29
]
.

A general cross algorithm
computes the following TT-representation,
see Subsection
4.2
, the definition Eq. (
43
), and in particular
Eq. (
44
):

f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M})=\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{r_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{r_{2}}\cdots\sum_{s_{M-1}=1}^{r_{M-1}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}^{(1)}_{s_{0},s_{1}}(\alpha_{1})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}^{(2)}_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\alpha_{2})\cdots\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{v}}^{(M)}_{s_{M-1},s_{M}}(\alpha_{M}).

(67)

For analytic
f(\cdot)
the TT-ranks often depend only logarithmically on the
accuracy
[
72
,
108
]
.

These algorithms are an alternative to iterations and series expansions, they are
tailored specifically to low-rank approximations in a particular tensor format.
They allow one
to compute a low-rank approximation of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
for a given function
f(\cdot)
and a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}
directly, “on the fly”.
We assume here that the full tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}:=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
is not given explicitly, but rather as a function
which can return any element
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}}:=f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{i}}})
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
.

For example, the AMEn algorithm can compute the representation Eq. (
67
) using only
\mathcal{O}(dnr^{2})
entries of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}
and
\mathcal{O}(dnr^{3})
additional arithmetic operations.
The pseudocode is listed in
[
25
]
.
It is based on the skeleton decomposition (another name is adaptive cross approximation) of a matrix
[
47
,
9
,
8
]
,
and the
maxvol
algorithms
[
46
]
. The idea of the
maxvol
algorithms is to find a rank-
r
matrix approximation
A_{r}
of a
n\times m
matrix
A
, the rank of which is
r
.
The
maxvol
algorithm suggests to select among all
r\times r
submatrices the one that has the largest volume (determinant).
The computational complexity is
\mathcal{O}(r(n+m))
, and the approximation error
\|A-A_{r}\|_{\infty}\leq(r+1)\sigma_{r+1}
, where
\sigma_{r+1}
is the
(r+1)
-th singular value of
A
.

6
Numerical examples

This section shows the real applicability of tensor techniques addressed in Sections
2
–
5
.
This includes a validation Example
6.1
, where the KLD is computed with a
well known analytical formula and the AMEn
\_
cross algorithm

[
32
,
107
]
from the TT-toolbox for
d
-dimensional Gaussian
pdfs
.
Further, in Example
6.2
the Hellinger distances
are computed with the well known analytical formula as well as with the AMEn
\_
cross algorithm.
To show the approach on a distribution where the
pdf
is not known analytically, the
d
-variate
elliptically contoured
\alpha
-stable distributions are chosen and accessed via their
pcfs
, and
again KL and Hellinger distances for different value of
d
,
n
, and the parameter
\alpha
are
computed (Example
6.3
).

For the numerical tests below we used the Matlab package
TT-Toolbox

[
100
]
,
which is well known in the tensor community. To compute
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
, we use the

alternating optimization with enrichment (AMEn)
method
[
32
]
, provided
in the TT-toolbox library. This is a block cross algorithm with an error-based enrichment.
It tries to interpolate the function
f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{w}})
via the error-enriched maxvol-cross method.
All computations are done on a MacBook Pro computer produced in 2018, equipped with a 6-Core
Intel Core i7 processor running at 2.2 GHz, and 16 GB RAM.
We started by computing the point-wise inverse, squared root, and exponent of a given discretised

pdf
represented as a TT tensor. For this, iterative methods, series expansions, and the AMEn
algorithm
[
32
,
107
]
were used in order to make sure
that the AMEn method gives the same results as other methods.

The first Example
6.1
is a validation example, where the analytical formula
for the KLD is known analytically. This exact value is compared with the approximate KLD
(denoted by
\widetilde{D}_{\text{KL}}
) for high values of
d
and
n
.
One may observe that they are almost the same.
Additionally, the absolute error (
\text{err}_{a}:=|D_{\text{KL}}-\widetilde{D}_{\text{KL}}|
)
as well as the relative error (
\text{err}_{r}:={|D_{\text{KL}}-\widetilde{D}_{\text{KL}}|}/{|D_{\text{KL}}|}
) and the computing times (last row) are shown.

Example 6.1

(Validation example 1 — KLD)
.

Consider two Gaussian distributions
\mathcal{N}_{1}:=\mathcal{N}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{1})
and

\mathcal{N}_{2}:=\mathcal{N}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{2})
, where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{1}:=\sigma_{1}^{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}
,

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{2}:=\sigma_{2}^{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}=(1.1\ldots,1.1)
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}=(1.4,\ldots,1.4)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

d=\{16,32,64\}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}
is the identity matrix, and
\sigma_{1}=1.5
,
\sigma_{2}=22.1
.
The well known analytical formula is
[
101
]

D_{\text{KL}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\|\mathcal{N}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tr{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{2}^{-1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{1})}+(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1})^{T}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{2}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1})-d+\log\left(\frac{|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{2}|}{|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{1}|}\right)\right).

(68)

After discretisation of the two
pdfs
, one obtains the tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}

(cf. Section
1
). Then the KLD
\widetilde{D}_{\text{KL}}
is computed as
in Table
2
. The results are summarised in Table
4
.

) for various values of

An important ingredient of the KLD computation is the
\log(\cdot)
function,
which can be computed
via the AMEn method, or the Gregory series Eq. (
65
).
We observed that the TT-ranks are increasing very fast in the Gregory series,
and it is not so transparent how and when to truncate them.
Therefore, we recommend using the AMEn algorithm (implemented in the TT Toolbox) for the approximation
\widetilde{D}_{\text{KL}}
by computing on the fly the function

f(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\odot(\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}})-\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}))
.
The small absolute (
\text{err}_{a}
) and relative (
\text{err}_{a}
) errors show that the
AMEn method can be used to compute the KLD.

The next validation test is with the Hellinger distance:

Example 6.2

(Validation example 2 — Hellinger distance)
.

For the Gaussian distributions from Example
6.1
,
the Hellinger distances computed via the AMEn algorithm—denoted
by
\widetilde{D}_{H}
—and the analytical formula Eq. (
69
) denoted by
D_{H}
)
are compared in Table
5
.
The squared Hellinger distances for two multi-variate Gaussian distributions can be computed
analytically as follows (see p.51 and p.45 in
[
101
]
):

D_{H}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2})^{2}=1-K_{{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2}),\quad\text{ where}

(69)

K_{{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2$}}}}}}}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2})=\frac{\det(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{1})^{{\mathchoice{{{{\raise ) for various values of

The results in Table
5
show that the AMEn algorithm is able to
compute the
D_{H}
Hellinger distance between two multivariate Gaussian distributions for
large dimensions
d=\{16,32,64\}
, and for large
n=2048
. The exact and approximate
values are identical, and the error is small. The absolute and relative errors
(
\text{err}_{a}
,
\text{err}_{r}
) can be further decreased by taking a smaller TT tolerance.

After these validation tests, we choose the
d
-variate
elliptically contoured
\alpha
-stable distribution, where no analytical formula
for the
pdf
is known, and which generalises the normal law. These distributions
have heavy tails and are often used for modelling
financial data
[
91
]
.
We access the distribution in the next Example
6.3
through its
pcf
, which is
known analytically Eq. (
71
).

Example 6.3

(
\alpha
-stable distribution)
.

The
pcf

of a
d
-variate elliptically contoured
\alpha
-stable distribution is given by

\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})=\exp\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{i}}{\textstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{i}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\mid\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}\right\rangle^{{\mathchoice{{{{\raise 1.80835pt\hbox{{$\textstyle\alpha$}}}{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\kern-1.05487pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-0.90417pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.1097pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}}}{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.60275pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 2.2604pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}{\raise 0.51234pt\hbox{\kern-1.35623pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.45206pt\hbox{\kern-1.20552pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}}}}}}}\right).

(71)

We approximate
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{t}})
as in Eq. (
7
), but in the TT format Eq. (
43
)
and Eq. (
67
). The tolerance used in the AMEn algorithm is
10^{-9}
. Further,
from the inversion theorem, the
pdf
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be computed as in
Eq. (
8
) via the FFT.

We start by computing the KLD between two
\alpha
-stable distributions for fixed
\alpha_{1}=2.0
,

\alpha_{2}=1.9
(with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}=0
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{C}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}
); the results are summarised in Table
6
.

From Table
6
one may see that
n=32
(for
d=16
) is sufficient, and there is
no need to a take higher resolution
n
, the KLD value is (almost) not changing.
One may also see that for
d=32
one needs to take
n=256
or higher, but a higher
n

requires more memory.

These values in the last column in Table
6
, namely
d=32
and
n=256
,
can be used to illustrate the amount of data and computation which would be involved in
a—here impossible—full representation. The values
d=32
and
n=256
mean that the amount of data
in full storage mode would be
N=n^{d}=265^{32}\approx 1.16\mathrm{E}77
,
and assuming 8 bytes per entry, this would be ca.
1\mathrm{E}78
bytes.
Compare this to the estimated number of hadrons in the universe (
1\mathrm{E}80
), to see that
alone the storage of such an object is not possible in full mode, whereas in a
TT-low-rank approximation it did not require more than ca.
626\mathrm{MB}
, and
fits on a laptop. And as for the computation of the KLD
shown in that table, assume that the computation of the logarithms per data point could
be achieved at a rate of
1\mathrm{GHz}
. Then the KLD computation in full mode would
require ca.
1.2\mathrm{E}68\mathrm{s}
, or more than
3\mathrm{E}60
years, and even with a
perfect speed-up on a parallel super-computer with say

1,000,000
processors, this would require still more than
3\mathrm{E}54
years; compare this
with the estimated age of the universe of ca.
1.4\mathrm{E}10
years.

A note about software.

Several tensor toolboxes developed for
low-rank tensor calculus are available.
The CP and Tucker decompositions are implemented in the Tensor Toolbox

[
5
,
6
,
1
]
, and in the Tensorlab

[
117
,
22
]
.
The TT and QTT tensor formats are implemented in
TT-toolbox

[
100
]
.
The hierarchical Tucker tensor format is realised in the Hierarchical Tucker Toolbox

htucker toolbox

[
81
]
.
For a more detailed overview, see
[
54
,
76
]
.
Almost all available implementations (e.g. TT-toolbox and htucker) are have been used to solve
(stochastic) PDEs
[
28
]
, integral equations, linear systems in
tensor format, or to perform arithmetic operations such as the scalar product, addition, etc.
But, to the best of our knowledge, we do not know any attempts for computing the KLD and other
divergences or distances, or the entropy of high-dimensional probability distributions.
The Matlab files from our computations are freely available at

https://github.com/litvinen/Divergence_in_tensors.git

7
Conclusion

The task considered here was the numerical computation of characterising statistics of
high-dimensional
pdfs
, as well as their divergences and distances,
where the
pdf
in the numerical implementation was assumed discretised on some regular grid.
Even for moderate dimension
d
, the full storage and computation with such objects become very quickly
infeasible.

We have demonstrated that high-dimensional
pdfs
,

pcfs
, and some functions of them
can be approximated and represented in a low-rank tensor data format.
Utilisation of low-rank tensor techniques helps to reduce the computational complexity
and the storage cost from exponential
\mathcal{O}(n^{d})
to linear in the dimension
d
, e.g.
\mathcal{O}\left(dnr^{2}\right)
for the TT format. Here
n
is the number of discretisation
points in one direction,
r\ll n
is the maximal tensor rank, and
d
the problem dimension.
The particular data format is rather unimportant,
any of the well-known tensor formats (CP, Tucker, hierarchical Tucker, tensor-train (TT)) can be used,
and we used the TT data format. Much of the presentation and in fact the central train
of discussion and thought is actually independent of the actual representation.

In the beginning in Section
1
it was motivated through three possible ways how one may
arrive at such a representation of the
pdf
. One was if the
pdf
was given in some approximate
analytical form, e.g. like a function tensor product of lower-dimensional
pdfs
with a
product measure, or from an analogous representation of the
pcf
and subsequent use of the
Fourier transform, or from a low-rank functional representation of a high-dimensional
RV, again via its
pcf
.
The theoretical underpinnings of the relation between
pdfs
and
pcfs
as well as their
properties were recalled in Section
2
, as they are important to be preserved in the
discrete approximation. This also introduced the concepts of the convolution and of
the point-wise multiplication Hadamard algebra, concepts which become especially important if
one wants to characterise sums of independent RVs or mixture models,
a topic we did not touch on for the sake of brevity but which follows very naturally from
the developments here. Especially the Hadamard algebra is also
important for the algorithms to compute various point-wise functions in the sparse formats.
The Section
2
, as well as the following Section
3
and Section
5
are
actually completely independent of any particular discretisation and representation of the data

Some statistics, divergences, and distance measures were collected in Section
3

together with the abstract discrete expressions on how to compute them numerically,
independent of any particular numerical representation.
To demonstrate our idea, one of the easiest tensor formats—the CP tensor format—was described
first in Section
4
. In the numerical part, we used the tensor-train (TT) format,
which is also sketched in Section
4
, together with how to implement the operations
of the Hadamard algebra.
As Section
3

shows, point-wise functions are required in order to compute the desired statistics,
and some algorithms to actually perform this in an algebra were collected in Section
5
.
These were originally developed for matrix algebra algorithms
[
64
]
, but they
work just as well in any other associative C
∗
-algebra. In using such algorithms,
one assumes that the ranks of the involved tensors are not increasing strongly
during iterations and linear algebra operations.

In the numerical computations in Section
6
, the first example
is one where the analytic answer was known, and this validates the approach
and shows the accuracy of the low-rank computation in the computation
of KL and Hellinger distances, which were
taken as examples of characterising functionals resp. statistics.
As a more taxing problem, we then took elliptically contoured
\alpha
-stable distributions
to evaluate the KLD and Hellinger distances between them. For these distributions,
the
pdf
is not known analytically, but the
pcf
is, which we took as our
starting point.
The AMEn TT-Cross algorithm
[
24
,
25
]
was
used to compute the low-rank approximations, and the
pdfs
were then computed via FFT.
This also nicely demonstrates the smooth integration of the FFT
into low-rank tensor formats, and
made it subsequently possible to compute the required quantities.

In total, these examples showed the viability of this concept, namely that it is possible to
numerically operate on discretised versions of high-dimensional distributions
with a reasonable computational expense, whereas in a full format the computations
would not have been feasible at all.
All that we required was that the data in its discretised form can be considered
as an element of a commutative C
∗
-algebra with an inner product,
where the algebra operations only have to be numerically computed in an
approximative fashion.
Such an algebra is isomorphic to a commutative sub-algebra
of the usual matrix algebra, allowing the use of matrix algorithms.
